### 3.3.2. Les CMA dans les déserts.

De par les nombreux bénéfices apportés à la plante, par la symbiose mycorhizienne, la mycorhize arbusculaire peut aider les plantes à tolérer et se développer dans les conditions désertiques. En effet la mycorhize permet de récupérer de l’eau et des nutriments faiblement disponibles, pour les transmettre à la plante, mais aussi d’aider à tolérer les stress oxydatifs (salins et hydriques), de chaleur et également les stress biotiques (Apple 2010 ; Duc et al. 2018 ; Vasar et al. 2021). Dans les déserts, la capacité des mycorhizes à aider à structurer les sols, est particulièrement utile dans les sols sableux instables et participe à fixer les dunes (Rillig et Mummey 2006). [SOURCE: Page_112]

D’un autre côté, les conditions de sécheresse très fortes, la très haute salinité, la chaleur intense, le faible couvert végétal, et l’abrasivité des sols sont autant de facteurs délétères pour les champignons mycorhiziens, les plantes représentent donc des niches refuges (Apple 2010 ; Vasar et al. 2021 ; Adenan et al. 2021). Il est à penser aussi que le coût en carbohydrates pour la plante puisse dépasser les bénéfices tirés par la mycorhize dans ces conditions extrêmes (la photosynthèse étant consommatrice d’eau et le flux de nutriments vers la plante pouvant être limité par l’absence d’eau ; Vasar et al. 2021). Ainsi, dans les déserts, les proportions de plantes non mycorhizées sont souvent plus élevées que dans les autres environnements (Selosse and Le Tacon 1998 ; Soudzilovskaia et al. 2017 ; Brundrett and Tedersoo 2018). L’intensité et la fréquence de colonisation des racines est faible chez les plantes désertiques puisque cette colonisation dépend du nombre de racines fines qui est plus faible en milieu désertique (Treseder et Cross 2006). De plus les spores sont généralement peu nombreuses dans les sols d’environnements désertiques (Vasar et al. 2021). [SOURCE: Page_112]

49 [SOURCE: Page_112]

Les déserts montrent un fort endémisme également chez les CMA (Niskanen et al. 2023), ainsi malgré le faible nombre de taxons estimés chez les CMA, de nouveaux taxons sont encore identifiés régulièrement, notamment dans les déserts (Symanczik et al. 2014a, b). Les communautés de CMA dépendent de la plante hôte mais également fortement des conditions environnementales, notamment édaphiques (Adenan et al. 2021). La température et le pH, mais aussi la disponibilité en nutriments joue des rôles majeurs dans l’organisation et la sélection des communautés (Rodríguez- Echeverría et al. 2017 ; Davison et al. 2021). [SOURCE: Page_114]

Taxonomiquement, les CMA de la famille des Glomeraceae étaient les plus souvent retrouvés par metabarcoding (Senés-Guerrero et al. 2020 ; Adenan et al. 2021 ; Guardiola-Márquez et al. 2022) et par identification de spores (López-Sánchez et Honrubia 1992 ; Carballar-Hernández et al. 2013 ; Albaqami et al. 2018) à la fois en environnements naturels et en agrosystèmes. D’autres familles ont aussi      pu   être   identifiées,   par   exemple   des   Claroideoglomeraceae,    Acaulosporaceae, Entrophosporaceae, Gigasporaceae, Diversisporaceae et Archaeosporaceae. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Pour les agrosystèmes en milieux désertiques, ceux-ci sont souvent dominés par la culture du palmier dattier. [SOURCE: Page_114]

3.4.          Les communautés fongiques des racines du palmier dattier 3.4.1. Les racines de dattier et ses champignons [SOURCE: Page_114]

Dans les environnements désertiques, le palmier dattier est la plante la plus emblématique et la plus importante sur le plan agronomique. En raison de son importance, les scientifiques se sont particulièrement intéressés aux communautés fongiques qui lui sont associées, car ces communautés jouent un rôle crucial dans la survie et les rendements de cette plante cultivée (Abumaali et al. 2023 ; Ben Zineb et al. 2024). [SOURCE: Page_114]

Les taxons et communautés de champignons endophytes des racines de palmier dattier ont pu être analysées par des méthodes de culture et d’analyse moléculaires. Ben Chobba et al. (2013), par exemple, dans des palmiers en Tunisie, ont isolé de racines deux souches de Fusarium (Fusarium sp. et lacertarum) tandis que par méthode moléculaire (Restriction Fragment Length Polymorphism) ils ont identifié des taxons assignés à des Alternaria, Bipolaris et Pythium. [SOURCE: Page_114]

L’effet des paramètres caractéristiques des régions arides sur le mycobiome du dattier a été étudié. La salinité représente une menace pour la phoeniciculture et celle-ci est croissante dans les eaux d’irrigations. Ce paramètre a été testé en serre sur de jeunes plantes par Yaish et al. (2017) qui ont identifiés 24 OTUs fongiques (e.g. Fusarium, Humicola, Aspergillus). Parmi celles-ci seuls deux taxons [SOURCE: Page_114]

50 [SOURCE: Page_114]

étaient différentiellement exprimés en condition saline (augmentation de Humicola fuscoatra et diminution de Aspergillus niger). En palmeraies, des conditions de salinités différentes et leurs effets sur les communautés fongiques ont aussi été étudiées. La diversité fongique dans les racines était plus faible en conditions saline chez Chandran et al. (2024) et les compositions des communautés étaient impactées par la salinité. Dans leur étude, les auteurs soulèvent l’importance des processus stochastiques dans la construction des communautés fongiques endophytes des racines de dattier mais aussi l’impact de la salinité et du pH de l’eau d’irrigation. Yaish et al. (2024) soulèvent aussi l’importance des processus stochastiques mais aussi de la salinité dans la composition de ces communautés. Cette étude teste deux cultivars, un sensible à la salinité (Zabad) et un tolérant (Umsila). Les communautés étaient similaires en milieu non-salin, cependant en conditions salines les deux cultivars ont montré des communautés fongiques différentes, indiquant que les cultivars influencent différemment leurs communautés en conditions de stress salin. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Cette étude soulève la question de l’effet du cultivar sur les communautés microbiennes, en effet les cultivars de dattier sont souvent sélectionnés en fonction de leurs fruits mais souvent également, de manière consciente ou inconsciente, en fonction de leur tolérance aux stress salins. Al-Hadidi et al. (2024) notent également l’importance du cultivar mais aussi de la localisation géographique sur les communautés fongiques. Parmi les différents génotypes étudiés, certains sont des dattiers en milieu naturel, féraux. Ces dattiers féraux montraient la plus grande proportion de taxons spécifiques (non partagés avec les autres génotypes) dans leurs rhizosphères. Cette étude s’intéressait également à l’utilisation de fertilisants chimiques et de biofertilisants et conclue que la plus grande diversité fongique dans la rhizosphère était visible avec l’apport conjoint de biofertilisants et d’engrais chimiques. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Les communautés fongiques sont également étudiées dans le cadre des stress biotiques, sur des palmiers infectés par des pathogènes. Au sein de ces pathogènes fongiques, une sous-espèce en particulier attire beaucoup l’attention : Fusarium oxysporum f. sp. albedinis. Ce champignon provoque la maladie du Bayoud, une maladie du palmier dattier qui a dévasté les cultures en Afrique du Nord, notamment au Maroc et en Algérie (Jaiti et al. 2007). Cependant, il n'a pas encore été détecté en Arabie saoudite, qui applique des mesures de contrôle et de surveillance strictes (Saleh et al. 2015). L’incidence de cette maladie a été diminuée en condition contrôlées par l’addition de biofertilisant et de phosphate (Anli et al. 2022). Une autre maladie du palmier dattier, « La maladie des feuilles cassantes » (liée à une carence en manganèse et la présence probable d’un pathogène ; Triki et al. 2003) a également été étudiée par Mefteh et al. (2017). Leur étude montre des abondances de taxons fongiques différentes entre les racines de dattiers malades et sains. Cette étude a également mis en avant la production par ces communautés de métabolites secondaires pouvant jouer des rôles [SOURCE: Page_116]

51 [SOURCE: Page_116]

antibactériens et antifongiques. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Cette notion est également discutée par Mohamed Mahmoud et al. (2017) sur des dattiers en dunes costales. Cette étude a identifié par culture des taxons endophytes fongiques dans les racines, ces taxons étaient majoritairement des Hypocreales (Fusarium, Clonostachys) mais aussi des Eurotiales (Aspergillus, Penicillium). [SOURCE: Page_118]

Globalement, les taxons endophytes les plus cités dans les études sont des Fusarium, Aspergillus, Penicillium, Alternaria et Glomus (Abumaali et al. 2023). Au sein des communautés d’endophytes, les CMA jouent également un rôle important chez le palmier dattier et ont été abondamment étudiés. [SOURCE: Page_118]

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Summary: Technical section focusing on 3.4.2. les cma chez le palmier dattier
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 3.4.2. Les CMA chez le palmier dattier

Étant donné les conditions de vie du dattier, dans des sols avec de faibles concentrations en nutriments et en matière organique, une forte salinité et de possibles intenses stress hydriques, la mycorhize arbusculaire bénéficie potentiellement fortement au dattier (Al-Karaki 2013). En effet le palmier dattier est une plante assez largement décrite comme étant mycorhizée en conditions naturelles. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Les spores de CMA dans la rhizosphère de dattier a été étudiée en Afrique du Nord, dans plusieurs régions, et par plusieurs auteurs. Au Maroc, les nombres de spores ont été mesurés entre 331 et 18,400 spores pour 100 g de sols (Bouamri et al. 2006, 2014 ; Harkousse et al. 2021b ; Gagou et al. 2023). Bouamri et al. (2014) lient la densité de spores aux précipitations pour expliquer une variabilité saisonnière du nombre de spores. Cette variabilité saisonnière dans les densités de spores a aussi été mesurée par Zougari-elwedi et al. (2016) qui ont mesuré entre 100 spores / 100 g au printemps et en été et 450 en automne. En Algérie, Khirani et al. (2020) dénombraient entre 150 et 345 spores / 100 g entre différentes localisations. Une étude a mesuré en Arabie saoudite cette densité de spores et a compté entre 392 et 548 spores / 100 g (Al-Yahya’ei et al. 2011). Le nombre de spores dans la rhizosphère du dattier dépend donc de la localisation, de l’humidité des sols et de la phénologie de la plante (Bouamri et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_118]

Les fréquences et intensités de mycorhization ont été mesurées également dans les jardins de palmeraies. Tous les dattiers rapportés dans les études ont été retrouvées mycorhizées. Les fréquences de mycorhization oscillent entre 72,5 et 100% chez Bouamri et al. (2006), et presque 0% en hiver (82% en automne) chez (Zougari-elwedi et al. 2016). Des inocula faits à partir de rhizosphères de dattier a permis de mycorhizer d’autres plantes (e.g. maïs, orge, sorgho ; Al-Yahya’ei et al. 2011 ; Bouamri et al. 2014 ; Harkousse et al. 2021b ; Gagou et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_118]

En ce qui concerne les taxons détectés comme étant associés au dattier en conditions cultivées, les morphotypages de spores ont permis d’identifier une majorité de Glomus dans quasiment toutes les [SOURCE: Page_118]

52 [SOURCE: Page_118]

études (Bouamri et al. 2006, 2014 ; Al-Yahya’ei et al. 2011 ; Zougari-elwedi et al. 2016 ; Harkousse et al. 2021b ; Gagou et al. 2023). D’autres taxons tels que Acaulospora et Scutellospora ont également été fréquemment observés, mais aussi, Sclerocystis, Diversispora, Entrophospora, Funneliformis, Racocetra, Claroideoglomus, Paraglomus, Ambispora et même Gigaspora. Au metabarcoding, avec le marqueur ITS$_2$, Chandran et al. (2024) ont repéré très peu de séquences liées aux CMA, les quelques- unes détectées étaient assignées au genre Glomus. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Les CMA sont beaucoup étudiés pour leurs capacités à aider la plante à tolérer les stress. Ainsi de nombreuses études cherchent à étudier l’effet de l’inoculation par des champignons mycorhiziens sur cette tolérance. Le paramètre de sécheresse et de manque d’eau en général est très étudié sur le palmier dattier étant donné la problématique de la ressource en eau dans les régions cultivatrices. La sécheresse diminue la colonisation des racines de jeunes plantes de dattiers par les CMA (Baslam et al. 2014) mais la mycorhization augmente la teneur relative en eau dans les feuilles et augmentait régulièrement la croissance de la plante en conditions de stress hydrique, dépendant de la souche utilisée dans l’inoculum. Les mêmes conclusions ont été obtenues par (Meddich et al. 2015 ; Harkousse et al. 2021b ; Anli et al. 2022) et (Benhiba et al. 2015) avec également une augmentation d’enzymes (peroxidase, polyphenolxydases, catalase, superoxide dismutase, ascorbate peroxidase, et guaiacol peroxidase) liées à la lutte contre le stress oxydatifs chez les plants mycorhizés montrant que la mycorhization aide la plante à mieux résister aux stress oxydatifs provoqués par le stress hydrique. Les résultats dépendent beaucoup du partenaire fongique utilisé, les études testent régulièrement des inoculums monospécifiques ou bien des mélanges d’espèces. Ces inocula sont parfois également testés en combinaison avec des bactéries PGPR (Plant Growth Promoting Rhizobacteria) ou à des biofertilisants (Harkousse et al. 2021b ; Anli et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_120]

Les mêmes conclusions ont également été obtenues en conditions de salinité, la salinité étant également un stress osmotique, une partie des réponses de la plante est la même (Figure 20 ; Diatta et al. 2014 ; Ait-El-Mokhtar et al. 2019, 2021). Globalement, à chaque fois, et ce peu importe les conditions, l’inoculation a permis d’augmenter la croissance des dattiers (Baslam et al. 2014 ; Meddich et al. 2015 ; Anli et al. 2020, 2022 ; Harkousse et al. 2021b). [SOURCE: Page_120]

Enfin, la mycorhize arbusculaire a également été étudiée pour sa capacité à aider le dattier à tolérer la maladie du Bayoud. Les jeunes plantes infectées ont montré une moins forte sévérité de la maladie, en augmentant l’activité d’enzymes de défenses : peroxidases et polyphenoloxidases (Jaiti et al. 2007 ; Abohatem et al. 2011). [SOURCE: Page_120]

53 [SOURCE: Page_120]

Objectifs de la these Cette thèse a pour objectif de proposer des moyens de réduire la consommation en eau de la culture du palmier dattier dans la région hyperaride d’AlUla en Arabie saoudite. Étant donné l’importance des communautés fongiques dans les cycles géochimiques et dans la nutrition minérale et hydrique des plantes, notamment du dattier, cet axe d’étude a été choisi pour chercher des solutions pour réduire la quantité d’eau nécessaire à la phoeniciculture. Ces paramètres chez le dattier ont, entre autres, été étudiés au regard des systèmes d’irrigations dont la compréhension est capitale pour atteindre des objectifs d’économie de l’eau. Cette étude s’intéresse également à la mycorhization arbusculaire, du palmier dattier dans le contexte de la région d’AlUla. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Dans le chapitre 1, nous avons commencé par dresser un état des communautés de champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules dans les interactions sols-plantes à l’échelle de la région d’AlUla (Robin- Soriano et al. 2024a). Pour cela nous avons utilisé un très grand nombre d’échantillons, de sols et de racines prélevés in situ tout au long du projet SoFunLand. Ces échantillons ont été analysés par : 1 – l’extraction des spores du sol rhizosphérique, l’observation de la colonisation des racines par les champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules et le metabarcoding de séquences ITS et 18S des sols et racines de plantes. Nous avons ainsi pu décrire ces communautés de champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules dans la grande variabilité de conditions, d’usages de terres et de plantes de la région. Cet état des lieux nous permet d’abord de mieux comprendre et appréhender cette symbiose à l’échelle d’une région, dans des écosystèmes arides et hyperalcalins, mais aussi de connaître les communautés de champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules qui peuvent interagir avec le palmier dattier. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Le chapitre 2, vise à caractériser l’effet de différents systèmes hydriques du sol : deux systèmes d’irrigations, goutte à goutte et flooding, ainsi qu’une condition abandonnée et une autre en milieu naturel, sur les communautés endophytiques des racines du palmier dattier (Robin-Soriano et al. 2024b). Cette étude vise également à analyser l’effet de la profondeur sur ces les communautés des racines. Pour cela, huit fosses d’1 m 60 de profondeur ont été ouvertes à 2 m de la base du tronc de palmiers dans les différentes conditions hydriques et des prélèvements de racines ont été faits à 3 profondeurs. Ces échantillons ont été complétés par d’autres palmiers prélevés à 40 cm à travers la région. Les racines ont été analysées par metabarcoding pour décrire les communautés fongiques, notamment les communautés de champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules. Ce chapitre permet d’étudier également l’architecture racinaire, encore mal connue du dattier, et comment les paramètres hydriques influent sur cette architecture racinaire. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Dans le chapitre 3 nous avons analysé plus précisément la mycorhization arbusculaire des palmiers dattiers de la région d’AlUla dans les différentes conditions dans lesquels ils peuvent être retrouvés. [SOURCE: Page_122]

54 [SOURCE: Page_122]

Cette partie se base sur l’analyse des racines de plus de 100 dattiers collectés à travers toute la région d’AlUla, qui sont comparés à des plantes compagnes collectées aux pieds des ainsi qu’avec des dattiers collectés dans une oasis distante de 200 km Khaybar. Ce chapitre permet d’analyser les différents paramètres, de culture (type de ferme, systèmes hydriques du sol), mais aussi géochimiques (pH, abondances atomiques) retrouvés dans la région et leu possible impact sur les chances de mycorhization du dattier. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Chapitre 1 [SOURCE: Page_144]

1. Contexte de l’étude Ma thèse a débuté en Octobre 2021 dans le cadre du projet SoFunLand (Knowing Soil for a better understanding of its FUNctioning to improve LAND uses and preservation at AlUla), dont les premières campagnes de prélèvement ont eu lieu dès 2019. Durant ces années et celles qui ont suivi le début de ma thèse, de nombreux prélèvements ont été réalisés, avec différents objectifs, dans une grande diversité d’environnements, de conditions et d’espèces de plantes. Il s’est vite révélé au début de ma thèse que la question de la mycorhize arbusculaire allait être centrale dans mon étude. Pourtant les conaissances sur la mycorhize dans les déserts restent relativement minces. Avant de se plonger dans l’analyse détaillée du dattier, nous avons donc vu l’occasion d’exploiter la masse d’échantillons et de données collectés durant ce projet pour comprendre à grande échelle quel était l’état de la mycorhize arbusculaire à l’échelle d’une région désertique entière, la région d’AlUla. [SOURCE: Page_144]

De nombreux jeux de données ont donc été exploités pour cet article, analysés avec trois différentes méthodes : d’extraction de spores, d’histologie et de metabarcoding pour décrire la mycorhize arbusculaire en tant que symbiose, en tant que propagules dans le sol et en tant que séquences d’ADN aussi bien dans des plantes que dans les sols. [SOURCE: Page_144]

Parmi ces jeux de données, une très grande quantité de plantes et de sols ont été collectés dans la réserve naturelle de Sharaan pour du metabarcoding et de l’histologie dans le cadre de la thèse de Kenji Maurice. Un second jeu de donnée, particulièrement conséquent est le jeu de donnée dit de la « Grille kilométrique ». Ce jeu de donnée consiste en un prélèvement de sol tout les kilomètres sur une grille de 1039 km² englobant la région d’AlUla dans sa grande diversité de conditions. Ce protocole de prélèvement a été inspiré par celui de l’Atlas Français des bactéries du Sol qui a cartographié le territoire Français métropolitain a raison d’un point de prélèvement tous les 16 kilomètres. Ces jeux de données s’ajoutent au prélèvement d’un grand nombre d’espèces végétales à travers la région, en écosystèmes naturels et en fermes (réalisés en dehors du cadre de ma thèse) et de prélèvements de palmiers dattiers et de plantes compagnes (pour cette thèse). [SOURCE: Page_144]

Dans cette étude nous pouvons donc analyser, à l’échelle d’une région et avec une résolution fine la mycorhize arbusculaire dans la diversité de ses composantes, ainsi que dans une variété d’environnements, de conditions et d’usages des terres. [SOURCE: Page_144]

74 [SOURCE: Page_144]

2. Article 1 : Absence of Gigasporales and rarity of spores in a hot desert revealed by a multimethod approach [SOURCE: Page_146]

Cet article a été publié dans le journal Mycorrhiza le 18 Juillet 2024. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Graphical Abstract de l’article 1. [SOURCE: Page_146]

## RESEARCH

Absence of Gigasporales and rarity of spores in a hot desert revealed by a multimethod approach Alexandre Robin-Soriano^1 · Kenji Maurice^1 · Stéphane Boivin^2 · Amelia Bourceret^3 · Liam Laurent-Webb^3 · Sami Youssef^2 · Jérôme Nespoulous^2 · Inès Boussière^1 · Julie Berder^2 · Coraline Damasio^1 · Bryan Vincent^1 · Hassan Boukcim^2,5 · Marc Ducousso^1 · Muriel Gros-Balthazard^4 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Received: 14 March 2024 / Accepted: 29 June 2024 © The Author(s), under exclusive licence to Springer-Verlag GmbH Germany, part of Springer Nature 2024 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Abstract Hot deserts impose extreme conditions on plants growing in arid soils. Deserts are expanding due to climate change, thereby increasing the vulnerability of ecosystems and the need to preserve them. Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) improve plant fitness by enhancing plant water/nutrient uptake and stress tolerance. However, few studies have focused on AMF diversity and community composition in deserts, and the soil and land use parameters affecting them. This study aimed to comprehensively describe AMF ecological features in a 5,000 km^2 arid hyperalkaline region in AlUla, Saudi Arabia. We used a multimethod approach to analyse over 1,000 soil and 300 plant root samples of various species encom- passing agricultural, old agricultural, urban and natural ecosystems. Our method involved metabarcoding using 18S and ITS$_2$ markers, histological techniques for direct AMF colonization observation and soil spore extraction and observation. Our findings revealed a predominance of AMF taxa assigned to Glomeraceae, regardless of the local conditions, and an almost complete absence of Gigasporales taxa. Land use had little effect on the AMF richness, diversity and community composition, while soil texture, pH and substantial unexplained stochastic variance drove these compositions in AlUla soils. Mycorrhization was frequently observed in the studied plant species, even in usually non-mycorrhizal plant taxa (e.g. Amaranthaceae, Urticaceae). Date palms and Citrus trees, representing two major crops in the region, however, displayed a very low mycorrhizal frequency and intensity. AlUla soils had a very low concentration of spores, which were mostly small. This study generated new insight on AMF and specific behavioral features of these fungi in arid environments. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Keywords Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi · Desert · Arid lands · Saudi Arabia · Metabarcoding · Hyperalkaline soils [SOURCE: Page_148]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_148]

Hot arid and hyperarid environments encompass approxi- mately 19% of the Earth’s terrestrial surface (Laity 2009) and are currently expanding as a result of climate change (IPCC 2022). 3. Supplementary materials 1.1.         Supplementary methodology 3.1.1. Metabarcoding datasets, library preparation and sequencing [SOURCE: Page_170]

Two protocols were used for root and soil sample preparation for DNA extraction: 1) root samples [SOURCE: Page_170]

were rinsed with sterile water, flash frozen in liquid nitrogen, and ground with a ceramic ball for three [SOURCE: Page_170]

30 s cycles at 5.0 speed in a FastPrep-24™ 5G instrument from MP Biomedicals™ (Irvine, California, [SOURCE: Page_170]

USA); 2) for the soil samples, a 5 mL volume of soil was added to 20 mL of sterile water, agitated, [SOURCE: Page_170]

centrifuged for 5 min at 12,000 g and the pellet was used for extraction in order to enhance the DNA [SOURCE: Page_170]

concentration. Soil and root DNA were extracted using the FastDNA Spin Kit for Soil (MP Biomedicals, [SOURCE: Page_170]

Solon, USA), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. DNA concentrations were assessed with the [SOURCE: Page_170]

Quant-iT™ PicoGreen™ fluorophore (dsDNA Assay Kits, ThermoFisher scientific, USA) using a TECAN [SOURCE: Page_170]

Spark multi-mode reader (TECAN, Switzerland). ITS86F (5′ GTGAATCATCGAATCTTTGAA 3′) and ITS$_4$ (5′ [SOURCE: Page_170]

TCCTCCGCTTATTGATATGC 3′) primers were used to amplify the fungal ITS$_2$ region (Op de Beeck et al. [SOURCE: Page_170]

2014)      for     both   the   Kilometer    Grid   and     SPS    dataset    samples.     AMADf      (5′ [SOURCE: Page_170]

GGGAGGTAGTGACAATAAATAAC 3′) and AMADGr (5′ CCCAACTATCCCTATTAATCAT 3′) primers were [SOURCE: Page_170]

used to amplify the 18S rRNA gene (Berruti et al. 2017) in the SPS and Palm dataset samples. The PCR [SOURCE: Page_170]

conditions and the purification of ITS$_2$ and 18S PCR products using AmpureXP beads (Beckman Coulter, [SOURCE: Page_170]

USA) were fully described in Maurice et al. (2023). Samples from the SPS dataset amplified with the [SOURCE: Page_170]

18S and ITS$_2$ markers are hereafter named SPS 18S and SPS ITS$_2$, respectively. Biological material in [SOURCE: Page_170]

the Kilometer Grid dataset (593 samples) and the SPS dataset (689 samples) were sequenced in two [SOURCE: Page_170]

independent Illumina NovaSeq runs by the Fasteris SA company (Switzerland). The 21 Palm dataset [SOURCE: Page_170]

samples were sequenced in one Illumina MiSeq run (2 x 250 bp), by the Fasteris SA company [SOURCE: Page_170]

(Switzerland). [SOURCE: Page_170]

Details on the data processing pipeline are available in Perez-Lamarque et al. (2022). Operational [SOURCE: Page_170]

taxonomic units (OTUs) were formed in three steps: i) all sequences were merged, ii) chimeras were [SOURCE: Page_170]

removed after a quality check, and iii) the resulting sequences were clustered into operational [SOURCE: Page_170]

96 [SOURCE: Page_170]

taxonomic units (OTUs) with 97% similarity. Negative controls incorporated from the DNA extraction [SOURCE: Page_171]

to PCR were used to decontaminate the OTU tables using decontam. Short sequences and sequences [SOURCE: Page_171]

assigned to non-fungal species were removed. In the Grid and SPS datasets, OTUs with < 100 reads [SOURCE: Page_171]

were removed. In the Palm dataset, OTUs with < 10 reads were removed because of the sequencing [SOURCE: Page_171]

depth differences between NovaSeq and MiSeq. After decontamination and cleaning, the Illumina [SOURCE: Page_171]

NovaSeq for SPS ITS$_2$ yielded 101,508,231 sequence reads and 17,028,263 reads for SPS 18S; the [SOURCE: Page_171]

NovaSeq for the Grid ITS$_2$ yielded 212,457,568 sequence reads, while the Miseq for Palm 18S yielded [SOURCE: Page_171]

326,001 sequence reads. Taxonomic assignment of OTUs was performed using the UNITE V$_8$.3 online [SOURCE: Page_171]

database for ITS$_2$ (Kõljalg et al. 2020) and the SILVA database for 18S (Quast et al. 2013). The UNITE [SOURCE: Page_171]

database was used as a reference for AMF taxonomy because it included the greatest number of AMF [SOURCE: Page_171]

genera, while all genera detected in SILVA were listed in UNITE. [SOURCE: Page_171]

All sequences generated in this study were deposited on the NCBI Sequence Read Archive (SRA) as [SOURCE: Page_171]

follows: i) the Kilometric Grid dataset sequences were deposited on the NCBI public repository [SOURCE: Page_171]

(PRJNA1058874), ii) the Palm dataset sequences were deposited on NCBI (PRJNA1078326). SPS data [SOURCE: Page_171]

sequences were retrieved from the NCBI public repository (PRJNA1061359). [SOURCE: Page_171]

---
### 3.1.2. Staining and observation of AMF in roots

Root staining was carried out in three steps: i) washing and clearing with a 10% KOH solution at 90°C [SOURCE: Page_171]

for 1 h, ii) rinsing and acidification with acidic water (acidified with diluted HCl at ~pH 3) for 2 h and iii) [SOURCE: Page_171]

Trypan blue staining in Amman lactophenol solution at 96°C for 10 min, according to the protocol of [SOURCE: Page_171]

Phillips and Hayman (1970). Roots were cut into 5 to 20 fragments of 1 cm, depending on the available [SOURCE: Page_171]

material. The symbiotic mycorrhizal status of the plant (mycorrhized or non-mycorrhized) was [SOURCE: Page_171]

confirmed when at least one fragment presented specific arbuscular mycorrhizae structures (vesicles [SOURCE: Page_171]

or arbuscules). When the AMF presence was confirmed and the root fragment number and quality [SOURCE: Page_171]

were sufficient, the mycorrhizal colonization frequency and intensity were assessed according to the [SOURCE: Page_171]

method of Trouvelot et al. (1986). The mycorrhizal frequency was calculated using the formula: [SOURCE: Page_171]

97 [SOURCE: Page_171]

(number of fragments with mycorrhizal structures/total number of fragments observed) *100. The [SOURCE: Page_172]

mycorrhizal intensity was measured with a percentage rating chart according to Trouvelot et al. (1986). [SOURCE: Page_172]

[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 4937810
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 3.1.3. amf spore extraction
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 3.1.3. AMF spore extraction

For each sampled soil, AMF spore extraction was performed on 1 kg of soil using the sieving and [SOURCE: Page_172]

decanting method (Gerdemann and Nicolson 1963), followed by 60% sucrose/water centrifugation. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Soil was sieved through a 2 mm Ø mesh and then resuspended in sterile water. After a 5 to 10 s [SOURCE: Page_172]

precipitation, particles were removed from the solution by sequentially filtering through a 1 mm Ø [SOURCE: Page_172]

mesh, sieve followed by a 40 µm Ø mesh sieve. Spores were collected between the two sieves (< 1 [SOURCE: Page_172]

mm, > 40 µm) and then transferred to Falcon© tubes half filled with sterile water. A 60% sucrose/water [SOURCE: Page_172]

solution was added to the bottom of the tube and the tubes were centrifuged for 2 min at 3,000 rpm. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Spores were collected at the sucrose-water interface. Total spore counting was performed for each [SOURCE: Page_172]

sample under a binocular magnifier to determine the number, size and shape of spores/kg. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Chapitre 2 [SOURCE: Page_180]

Contexte de l’étude Ce second chapitre permet de rentrer dans le cœur du sujet de cette thèse. On y parle directement de palmier dattier, de consommation d’eau et on s’interroge sur les moyens de réduire cette consommation en eau. [SOURCE: Page_180]

Ce chapitre s’appuie sur des prélèvements de palmiers dattiers collectés dans différentes conditions d’application de l’eau. Nous nous sommes surtout intéressés à deux systèmes d’irrigation : le traditionnel et consommateur flooding et le plus économe en eau goutte-à-goutte de surface. Un consensus assez fort affirme que les dattiers irrigués au goutte-à-goutte consomment moins d’eau, ont une meilleure croissance, de meilleurs rendements et une meilleure tolérance aux stress. Cependant très peu d’études se sont intéressées à comment les communautés microbiennes réagissent à ces différents systèmes d’irrigations et aucune sur le dattier, plante pourtant particulièrement concernée par le besoin d’économiser l’eau. Les communautés microbiennes jouent un rôle capital dans la santé et les performances agronomiques des plantes, mais peuvent être très sensibles aux conditions environnementales, notamment la disponibilité et la répartition de l’eau dans le sol. Cette question est donc à étudier de près lorsqu’on envisage, comme ici, des alternatives de systèmes de culture. Nous avons également observé deux systèmes non-irrigués : des fermes abandonnées, composés de dattiers ayant été irrigués mais qui ne le sont plus depuis au moins cinq années et des dattiers féraux en conditions naturels qui n’ont connu que la pluie et l’eau du sol depuis leur germination. Une partie des échantillons dans cette étude, deux dattiers par système hydrique ont fait l’objet d’un prélèvement particulier. Des fosses de 160 cm de profondeur, 200 cm de large et 80 cm de côté ont été creusées à deux mètres de l’axe du stipe du dattier ce qui a permis de récolter des racines à plusieurs profondeurs pour identifier de possibles différences de communautés fongiques en fonction de cette profondeur. Ces fosses ont également permis de décrire l’architecture racinaire du dattier sur un profil de 160 cm de profondeur, ce qui représente des données précieuses et inédites sur ce sujet peu référencé car difficiles à observer. [SOURCE: Page_180]

Le but de cette étude est de caractériser les communautés de champignons associées aux racines de palmier dattier dans ces différentes conditions, d’observer les différences entre systèmes d’irrigations et leur comportement en absence d’eau, en mettant également l’accent sur les communautés de CMA. Nous analysons également l’effet de la profondeur sur ces communautés. Nous étudions, enfin, ces paramètres sur l’architecture racinaire du dattier. Nous discutions également de l’effet des types d’exploitations sur la consommation en eau et sur les possibilités d’adaptations du système. [SOURCE: Page_180]

104 [SOURCE: Page_180]

Article 2: Digging deeper into the impacts of different soil water systems on the date palm root architecture and associated fungal communities [SOURCE: Page_182]

Cet article a été soumis au journal Symbiosis. [SOURCE: Page_182]

Graphical abstract de l’article 2. [SOURCE: Page_182]

105 [SOURCE: Page_182]

Digging deeper into the impacts of different soil water systems on the date palm root architecture and associated fungal communities [SOURCE: Page_184]

Authors: Alexandre Robin-Soriano^1, Bryan Vincent^1, Kenji Maurice^1, Liam Laurent-Webb^2, Vincent [SOURCE: Page_184]

Battesti^3, Hassan Boukcim^4,5, Marc Ducousso^1, Muriel Gros-Balthazard^6 [SOURCE: Page_184]

Corresponding author: Alexandre Robin-Soriano: [EMAIL_REDACTED] (institutional) [SOURCE: Page_184]

[EMAIL_REDACTED] (personal) ; ORCID: 0009-0003-8040-0062 [SOURCE: Page_184]

Affiliations: [SOURCE: Page_184]

1 AGAP-Institut, Univ Montpellier, CIRAD, INRAE, SupAgro, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_184]

2 ISYEB, Muséum national d’Histoire naturelle, CNRS, EPHE-PSL, Sorbonne Université, Paris, France [SOURCE: Page_184]

3 Lab Éco-anthropologie : CNRS, MNHN, Université Paris Cité, Paris, France [SOURCE: Page_184]

4 Department of Research and Development, VALORHIZ, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_184]

5 ASARI, Mohammed VI Polytechnic University, Laâyoune, Morocco [SOURCE: Page_184]

6 DIADE, Univ Montpellier, CIRAD, IRD, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_184]

Abstract: [SOURCE: Page_184]

In arid regions, excessive water use threatens agricultural sustainability and overall livelihoods. It is [SOURCE: Page_184]

essential to minimize water consumption to address these issues. Date palm (Phoenix dactylifera L.) is [SOURCE: Page_184]

an emblematic crop of arid regions and a major water consumer. Tailoring current irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_184]

into new, water-efficient systems could help cope with the water consumption of this crop. Microbial [SOURCE: Page_184]

communities associated with plants are essential for agricultural sustainability and could improve the [SOURCE: Page_184]

water use efficiency in regions threatened by water scarcity. These communities should thus be [SOURCE: Page_184]

seriously taken into account when adapting agrosystems to the current global change setting. [SOURCE: Page_184]

However, no information is presently available on the effects of these modifications on date palm [SOURCE: Page_184]

microbial communities. This study highlights the impact of different soil water systems (flooding and [SOURCE: Page_184]

106 [SOURCE: Page_184]

drip irrigation, natural conditions and abandoned farms) on date palm root fungal communities at [SOURCE: Page_185]

different soil depths. The findings revealed that the soil water systems had a marked impact on fungal [SOURCE: Page_185]

communities and that drip irrigation reduced the fungal diversity but increased the abundance of [SOURCE: Page_185]

arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi. We showed that these effects were similar at all sampling depths. Finally, [SOURCE: Page_185]

as the root architecture is a major determinant of water uptake, we reveal different behaviors of the [SOURCE: Page_185]

root architecture under these different soil water systems to 160 cm depth. The findings of this study [SOURCE: Page_185]

give new insights into the date palm root architecture and associated fungal communities, particularly [SOURCE: Page_185]

in the context of the water availability crisis, which drives the adaptation of agricultural systems. [SOURCE: Page_185]

Keywords: Desert; Date palm; Water scarcity; Irrigation system; Metabarcoding; Arbuscular [SOURCE: Page_185]

mycorrhizal fungi [SOURCE: Page_185]

1 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_186]

Non-renewable groundwater reservoirs are the major source of irrigation water in arid regions such as [SOURCE: Page_186]

Saudi Arabia (Odnoletkova and Patzek 2023). These aquifers are rapidly depleting because of their [SOURCE: Page_186]

naturally slow recharge rates combined with the high water extraction rates for agriculture (Shahin [SOURCE: Page_186]

and Salem 2014; Seraphin et al. 2022). This reliance on finite water resources significantly endangers [SOURCE: Page_186]

agricultural sustainability and overall livelihoods in such areas, thereby highlighting the critical need [SOURCE: Page_186]

for innovative water efficient management solutions. [SOURCE: Page_186]

The date palm (Phoenix dactylifera L.) is a major emblematic food crop of oasis agroecosystems in arid [SOURCE: Page_186]

North African and Southwest Asian regions. There are currently over 100 million date palm trees [SOURCE: Page_186]

worldwide (Khan et al. 2020). Date palms are of major cultural importance and a primary income [SOURCE: Page_186]

source in many countries (Hadrami and Al-Khayri 2012), especially in the main producing countries: [SOURCE: Page_186]

Egypt, Iran and Saudi Arabia (FAO data, 2021). This species is tolerant to abiotic stresses such as long- [SOURCE: Page_186]

term drought, soil and water salinity and heat which are common in these hot arid regions (Arab et al. [SOURCE: Page_186]

2016; Hazzouri et al. 2020). Yet irrigation is crucial to maintain high date palm growth rates and yields [SOURCE: Page_186]

(Carr 2012; Hazzouri et al. 2020), as reported for instance by Saad Eddin et al. (2023), who measured [SOURCE: Page_186]

a 30% date yield reduction when the irrigation water input was reduced from 100% to 60% of the total [SOURCE: Page_186]

water requirements of this crop. Date palms cultivation can consume over 13,000 m^3/ha/year of water [SOURCE: Page_186]

(Al-Omran et al. 2019) which can represent the highest proportions of water consumption at the scale [SOURCE: Page_186]

of arid countries like Saudi Arabia (Odnoletkova and Patzek 2023). The date palm cultivation, thus, [SOURCE: Page_186]

represents a critical target for reducing the agricultural irrigation footprint (Shahin and Salem 2014). [SOURCE: Page_186]

This cultivation occurs in a gradient of conditions ranging from conventional multispecies and [SOURCE: Page_186]

multicultivar farms to monoculture and monovarietal farms. Most studies carried out to investigate [SOURCE: Page_186]

water consumption and water-saving solutions in date palm stands have been focused on monoculture [SOURCE: Page_186]

farms. These studies identified two crucial points, i.e. the irrigation water quantity was too high and [SOURCE: Page_186]

the irrigation methods used resulted in excessive water loss (Carr 2012; Al-Omran et al. 2019). Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_186]

the amount of water applied often exceeds the date palm water requirements. Al-Omran et al. (2019) [SOURCE: Page_186]

108 [SOURCE: Page_186]

compared date palm water requirements to current irrigation conditions in Saudi Arabia using the [SOURCE: Page_187]

Penman–Monteith method for evapotranspiration and found that the water quantities actually [SOURCE: Page_187]

applied by farmers (12,714 m^3/ha/year) were much higher than the calculated needs (8342 [SOURCE: Page_187]

m^3/ha/year). Moreover, the yearly evapotranspiration of cultivated date palms measured by Ben Aissa [SOURCE: Page_187]

et al. (2019) was considered low, which was closely in line with the levels commonly noted in plant [SOURCE: Page_187]

species adapted to arid conditions, thereby implying that over-irrigation should not be needed. Hence, [SOURCE: Page_187]

current irrigation water quantities should be assessed and lowered to better match date palm needs [SOURCE: Page_187]

and prevent excessive water use. Another way to improve the date palm cultivation water efficiency [SOURCE: Page_187]

is to avoid water loss during irrigation (Carr 2012). This water loss may be partly due to overconsuming [SOURCE: Page_187]

irrigation practices. Historically, the most frequently used date palm irrigation method is called the [SOURCE: Page_187]

“flooding irrigation” method, which consists of bringing high amounts of water to the base of date [SOURCE: Page_187]

palm trees growing in an irrigation basin. In the past, this was many carried out via channels dug in the [SOURCE: Page_187]

soil between the basins, whereas this is currently often done via plastic pipes (Carr 2012). For example, [SOURCE: Page_187]

flooding was used to irrigate 90% of date palms in Egypt in 2008, and as high as 96% in Saudi Arabia in [SOURCE: Page_187]

2005 (Elfeky and Elfaki 2019). Many studies on monoculture date palm farms found that flooding [SOURCE: Page_187]

irrigation is outdated and inefficient compared to bubbler, sprinkler, and drip irrigation methods (Amiri [SOURCE: Page_187]

et al. 2007; Al-Amoud 2008; Carr 2012; Elfeky and Elfaki 2019). These studies highlighted that drip [SOURCE: Page_187]

irrigation, compared to flooding, can maintain or improve date yields, plant growth, tree health, and [SOURCE: Page_187]

soil water distribution patterns, (Al-Amoud 2008; Carr 2012; Elfeky and Elfaki 2019; Mohammed et al. [SOURCE: Page_187]

2021; Zemni et al. 2022) and may also affect date palm root distribution (Daddi Bouhoun 2010). For [SOURCE: Page_187]

example, in Saudi Arabia, the fields using drip irrigation consumed around 55 m^3/tree/year while fields [SOURCE: Page_187]

in fields irrigated by flooding received around 137 m^3/tree/year (Alazba 2004). Drip irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_187]

can be installed on the soil surface or buried underground, impacting the performance and water [SOURCE: Page_187]

distribution of the system (Talat Farid Ahmed 2012). In the present study, only surface drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_187]

was studied, so hereafter the term “drip irrigation” refers specifically to surface drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_187]

109 [SOURCE: Page_187]

The impacts of new irrigation systems like drip irrigation on crop yields and water efficiency in date [SOURCE: Page_188]

palm farms have been extensively explored (Al-Amoud 2010; Carr 2012; Elfeky and Elfaki 2019), but [SOURCE: Page_188]

the effects of different water management practices on root microbial communities are still unknown. [SOURCE: Page_188]

Yet microorganisms, and especially fungal communities have a significant impact on the health, [SOURCE: Page_188]

growth, yield and water and nutrient uptake of plants, including date palms (Ferjani et al. 2015; [SOURCE: Page_188]

Meddich et al. 2015; Abumaali et al. 2023). With the aim of boosting crop yields and agrosystem [SOURCE: Page_188]

sustainability, the roles of such fungal communities along the soil-plant continuum have been studied [SOURCE: Page_188]

in depth with regard to their ability to influence plant yields and tolerance to stresses (Xiong and Lu [SOURCE: Page_188]

2022). Among fungi, arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) are a prime example of a well-studied taxon [SOURCE: Page_188]

in agriculture. AMF are known to increase water and nutrient uptake in plants, improve their tolerance [SOURCE: Page_188]

to abiotic stresses, such as drought stress by increasing the anti-oxidative system, while also increasing [SOURCE: Page_188]

their tolerance to biotic stresses (Jaiti et al. 2007; Smith and Read 2010; Benhiba et al. 2015). In the [SOURCE: Page_188]

meantime, AMF are also sensible to environmental conditions that can affect their survival, [SOURCE: Page_188]

colonization capacities and community compositions. Determinants of AMF communities may be [SOURCE: Page_188]

environmental parameters such as pH, water and nutrient availability, plant phenology and root [SOURCE: Page_188]

architecture (Chourasiya et al. 2021), but also agricultural practices (Schreiner et al. 2007; Trinchera [SOURCE: Page_188]

and Warren Raffa 2023). Assessing how external factors (e.g. soil water systems) may drive [SOURCE: Page_188]

relationships between microbial communities, soil and plants could generate valuable insight to help [SOURCE: Page_188]

improve the sustainability of agricultural practices and soil management. [SOURCE: Page_188]

In one of the few studies carried out to assess the effects of drip versus flooding irrigation on fungal [SOURCE: Page_188]

communities, Deng et al. (2022) revealed a reduction in the abundance of arbuscular mycorrhizal and [SOURCE: Page_188]

ectomycorrhizal fungi under drip irrigation, alongside an increase in the abundance of pathogenic fungi [SOURCE: Page_188]

in the alfalfa rhizosphere in an arid region of China. The authors noticed an increase in soil nutrients [SOURCE: Page_188]

due to reduced leaching and that nitrogen and phosphorus availability strongly influenced the fungal [SOURCE: Page_188]

community compositions. These communities were thus also affected by the edaphic conditions, [SOURCE: Page_188]

notably the soil depth. Several studies have highlighted a change in the fungal community profile along [SOURCE: Page_188]

110 [SOURCE: Page_188]

a soil depth gradient in nutrient-rich soils in temperate environments (Lamit et al. 2017; Schlatter et [SOURCE: Page_189]

al. 2018). The authors of most of the studies noted that the fungal compositions generally differed [SOURCE: Page_189]

between the topsoil (first 25 cm) and the lower soil depths (Eilers et al. 2012; Gu et al. 2017; He et al. [SOURCE: Page_189]

2023a) in both arid and temperate environments, mainly due to the high biological activity and [SOURCE: Page_189]

differences in the physicochemical conditions at the surface. [SOURCE: Page_189]

In this study, we analyzed the effects of the soil water systems on fungal endophyte communities and [SOURCE: Page_189]

date palm root architecture parameters. [SOURCE: Page_189]

To overcome the knowledge gap regarding these effects on fungal communities in date palm roots, we [SOURCE: Page_189]

assessed the diversity and composition of fungal communities in date palm roots collected at different [SOURCE: Page_189]

sites in the desertic region of AlUla (al-ʿUlā: ‫)‬, Saudi Arabia. Our study focused on different soil [SOURCE: Page_189]

water systems (flooding and drip irrigation, abandoned farms and natural = uncultivated conditions) [SOURCE: Page_189]

and different soil depths (40, 80 and 140 cm deep). We hypothesized that the diversity and [SOURCE: Page_189]

composition of microbial communities would remain constant under both drip and flooding irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_189]

yet would differ significantly when compared to non-irrigated conditions (abandoned farms and [SOURCE: Page_189]

natural settings). As the soils in this region have only one soil horizon over tens of centimeters or even [SOURCE: Page_189]

meters deep, we assumed that physico-chemical properties would not change much vertically and [SOURCE: Page_189]

thus, the fungal communities would remain stable at all of the studied soil depths. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Moreover, we evaluated the contributions of the soil physicochemical parameters on the fungal [SOURCE: Page_189]

community composition. Finally, we assessed the impacts of different soil water systems on AMF [SOURCE: Page_189]

communities and taxa, as AMF are symbionts that provide substantial benefits to plants, including [SOURCE: Page_189]

improved water uptake in date palms (Qaddoury 2017). [SOURCE: Page_189]

The microbial communities in plant roots are closely linked to the root architecture (Saleem et al. [SOURCE: Page_189]

2018). However, the root architecture of full-grown date palms is generally unknown due to the [SOURCE: Page_189]

difficulty of monitoring the root system of such large trees (Amira and Ben Salah 2014). A few studies [SOURCE: Page_189]

have attempted to measure the root density of date palms in the 0 to 100 cm soil layer (Littardi et al. [SOURCE: Page_189]

2015; Tahri 2018), but this only encompasses a fraction of the date palm root system, which has been [SOURCE: Page_189]

111 [SOURCE: Page_189]

estimated to extend to at least 10 m deep, yet most of the roots are found within the top 2 m of the [SOURCE: Page_190]

soil layer (Krueger 2021). We hypothesized that the soil water systems could affect the root [SOURCE: Page_190]

development: (i) date palms watered with flooding and drip irrigation would not vary markedly with [SOURCE: Page_190]

regard to their root parameters as they are both irrigated, and (ii) drip irrigated date palms would have [SOURCE: Page_190]

an increased number of roots near the surface. Date palms growing on abandoned farms would have [SOURCE: Page_190]

a higher root width as these conditions were more long-standing than the irrigated conditions. Finally, [SOURCE: Page_190]

we hypothesized that uncultivated date palm trees collected in natural environments would have a [SOURCE: Page_190]

low number of roots throughout the studied soil profile as they could be more concentrated in layers [SOURCE: Page_190]

deeper than in those from which we collected our samples. [SOURCE: Page_190]

2 Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_190]

2.1 Study area [SOURCE: Page_190]

Our study was conducted in the hot arid desertic region of AlUla (al-‘Ulā oasis) in northwestern Saudi [SOURCE: Page_190]

Arabia. The climatic conditions in this hyperarid region are characterized by high temperatures, which [SOURCE: Page_190]

exceed 45°C in July and August, and rare, irregular rainfall events (30 mm to 170 mm/year) (Toumi et [SOURCE: Page_190]

al. 2015). The region hosts modern extensive farms and old or abandoned traditional farms, [SOURCE: Page_190]

encompassing different farming practices that were introduced during different periods (Gros- [SOURCE: Page_190]

Balthazard & Battesti et al. 2023). Agriculture in the AlUla region mainly relies on non-renewable water [SOURCE: Page_190]

supplies from the Saq-RAM Aquifer (Seraphin et al. 2022). In our study, date palms were sampled [SOURCE: Page_190]

under different soil water systems in four distinct environments or irrigation systems: (i) date palm [SOURCE: Page_190]

farms with flooding irrigation systems, (ii) date palm farms with surface drip irrigation systems, (iii) [SOURCE: Page_190]

abandoned date palms in old farms, where the crops were no longer irrigated or harvested, and (iv) [SOURCE: Page_190]

natural desert ecosystems hosting uncultivated, non-irrigated date palms that were located in the [SOURCE: Page_190]

Sharaan Nature Reserve (northeast of the city of AlUla). [SOURCE: Page_190]

112 [SOURCE: Page_190]

2.2 Sample collection [SOURCE: Page_191]

Overall, we collected and sampled the roots of 21 date palm trees across the target region under [SOURCE: Page_191]

different soil water systems. According to farmers and a previous survey carried out by Gros-Balthazard [SOURCE: Page_191]

& Battesti et al. (2023), the 14 date palm trees sampled in our study in flooding and drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_191]

conditions and in abandoned farms were all date palms of the Barnī cv., which meant they were [SOURCE: Page_191]

genetically identical. However, the genotypes of the 7 uncultivated date palms collected in natural [SOURCE: Page_191]

desert conditions were unknown. Date palm roots were collected in the four defined soil water [SOURCE: Page_191]

systems (flooding irrigation, drip irrigation, abandoned farms and natural environments) at various [SOURCE: Page_191]

depths (40 cm, 80 cm, 140 cm). Roots were collected manually using clean tweezers, placed in a 2% [SOURCE: Page_191]

cetrimonium bromide solution, and stored at 4°C until molecular analyses. For each sampling, soil was [SOURCE: Page_191]

collected a few centimeters from the roots for soil physicochemical analysis and sieved through a 2 [SOURCE: Page_191]

mm mesh sieve. Two methods were used to collect date palm roots: pit sampling at various depths (40 [SOURCE: Page_191]

cm, 80 cm, 140 cm), and additional diversity sampling at 40 cm depth. [SOURCE: Page_191]

Pit sampling [SOURCE: Page_191]

In October 2021, the “pit samples” were collected in two pits per soil water systems: (i) flooding [SOURCE: Page_191]

irrigation, (ii) drip irrigation, (iii) natural ecosystems, and (iv) abandoned farms, for a total of eight pits. [SOURCE: Page_191]

The pits were all 200 cm long, 80 cm wide, and 160 cm deep and were positioned 2 m from the trunk [SOURCE: Page_191]

and outside the irrigation basin, if applicable (Figs. 1a and 1b). We assessed the impact of the sampling [SOURCE: Page_191]

depth on the fungal communities after collecting roots in three soil horizons at 40-60 cm (low depth), [SOURCE: Page_191]

80-100 cm (medium depth) and 140-160 cm depth (high depth) (Figs. 1a and 1c). In each horizon, we [SOURCE: Page_191]

delineated three horizontal 65 cm long squares (Fig. 1d). In each square, 3 root segments [SOURCE: Page_191]

(preferentially lateral, small, fresh and live roots) were sampled and pooled in an Eppendorf tube. [SOURCE: Page_191]

There was a total of 9 samples per pit, i.e. three depths and three samples per depth. [SOURCE: Page_191]

Root density and root mean width were assessed based on photos taken of a quadrat placed on each [SOURCE: Page_191]

horizon (the whole horizon was imaged in two photos). The quadrat was 100 cm × 50 cm, subdivided [SOURCE: Page_191]

113 [SOURCE: Page_191]

into 10 cm² squares (Fig. 1d). Six photos were taken per pit, all of which were processed with the [SOURCE: Page_192]

ImageJ software to assess the root number and width. [SOURCE: Page_192]

The first four pit samples were collected from two farms located adjacently on either side of a road, [SOURCE: Page_192]

while the date palm trees were of similar age. Although the two farms were close together, their [SOURCE: Page_192]

irrigation systems differed, i.e. flooding irrigation for the first farm (pits N°1 and N°2), and drip [SOURCE: Page_192]

irrigation for the second farm (pits N°3 and N°4). To the best of our knowledge and according to [SOURCE: Page_192]

previous surveys, the drip irrigation system had been installed at least 3 years prior to the sampling. [SOURCE: Page_192]

Uncultivated date palm roots were sampled in the Sharaan Nature Reserve (pits N°5 and N°6). The last [SOURCE: Page_192]

pits (N°7 and N°8) were located in two old and abandoned farms, where the irrigation systems had not [SOURCE: Page_192]

been used for a long time prior to sampling, i.e., respectively 7 years and decades earlier, according to [SOURCE: Page_192]

local farmers. [SOURCE: Page_192]

Diversity sampling [SOURCE: Page_192]

Thirteen additional samples were collected in October 2021 and March 2022 around AlUla, always at [SOURCE: Page_192]

an easier to access, depth of 40 cm, to increase the number of samples and the size of the sampling [SOURCE: Page_192]

area. These samples are referred to as “Diversity samples”. Five of these samples were collected under [SOURCE: Page_192]

natural conditions in the Sharaan Nature Reserve, three were collected on farms under flooding [SOURCE: Page_192]

irrigation, three under drip irrigation and two in abandoned agrosystems. [SOURCE: Page_192]

The pit and diversity sampling locations are shown in Fig. 2. [SOURCE: Page_192]

114 [SOURCE: Page_192]

Fig. 1 Illustration of the pit sampling process. a. Pit location relative to the sampled date palm and the [SOURCE: Page_193]

pit sampling design. Adapted from (Munier 1973) b. Overview of a pit, the trunk of the sampled date [SOURCE: Page_193]

palm is on the left in the photo. c. Root sampling in a pit. d. Use of quadrat to measure the root number [SOURCE: Page_193]

and width. [SOURCE: Page_193]

115 [SOURCE: Page_193]

Fig. 2 Locations of the date palm root sampling sites throughout AlUla region. The samples were [SOURCE: Page_194]

collected by Pit sampling (180 cm deep) and Diversity sampling (40 cm deep). The date palms are [SOURCE: Page_194]

classified according to the soil water systems: flooding irrigation, drip irrigation, abandoned farms and [SOURCE: Page_194]

natural ecosystems. [SOURCE: Page_194]

2.3 Soil chemical analysis [SOURCE: Page_194]

For each soil, the pHH2O, pHKCl and acidification potential (AP = pHH2O - pHKCl) were measured, [SOURCE: Page_194]

complemented by the soil atomic element composition, i.e. ranging from magnesium to uranium, [SOURCE: Page_194]

determined by X-ray fluorescence using an XRF S$_1$ Titan analyzer (Bruker, Billerica, Massachusetts, [SOURCE: Page_194]

USA). The soils were prepared for pH measurement by adding H2O or a KCl solution (1 mol.L-1) to a [SOURCE: Page_194]

Falcon tube at 1:5 (v:v) ratio. After 1 h of mixing and decanting, the pH was measured with a pH meter [SOURCE: Page_194]

(Knick 766; Knick International, Berlin, Germany). For the XRF measurements, the soil samples were [SOURCE: Page_194]

placed in three plastic capsules, tamped and then sealed with Prolene film. Each capsule was measured [SOURCE: Page_194]

116 [SOURCE: Page_194]

in triplicate using the Geoexploration mode on the XRF device. For each soil, the nine measurements [SOURCE: Page_195]

were averaged and calibrated according to the limit of optical detection (LOD) of each analyzed [SOURCE: Page_195]

element (provided by Bruker). XRF provide the relative abundance of each measured element, they [SOURCE: Page_195]

are compositional by nature. They were thus analyzed by transforming the values using the centered [SOURCE: Page_195]

log ratio (clr) method so as to remove the closure effect inherent to compositional data (Reimann et [SOURCE: Page_195]

al. 2012). Only major elements that varied in the different samples were retained for further analysis: [SOURCE: Page_195]

magnesium (Mg), aluminum (Al), silicon (Si), phosphorus (P), potassium (K), calcium (Ca), iron (Fe), [SOURCE: Page_195]

sulfur (S) and manganese (Mn). [SOURCE: Page_195]

2.4 DNA extraction, library preparation and sequencing [SOURCE: Page_195]

The metabarcoding dataset used in this study was generated in our previous study (Robin-Soriano et [SOURCE: Page_195]

al. 2024), and previously deposited in the NCBI Sequence Read Archive under project number [SOURCE: Page_195]

PRJNA1078326. The DNA extraction, PCR, library preparation and sequencing methods were described [SOURCE: Page_195]

in Robin-Soriano et al. (2024). Briefly, roots were rinsed with sterile water, flash frozen and ground. [SOURCE: Page_195]

DNA was extracted with the FastDNA Spin Kit for Soil (MP Biomedicals, Solon, USA). The 18S rRNA [SOURCE: Page_195]

region was amplified with AMADf (5′ GGGAGGTAGTGACAATAAATAAC 3′) and AMADGr (5′ [SOURCE: Page_195]

CCCAACTATCCCTATTAATCAT 3′) primers (Berruti et al. 2017). All PCR products were sent to Fasteris SA [SOURCE: Page_195]

(Switzerland) for sequencing using an Illumina MiSeq (2 x 250 bp). [SOURCE: Page_195]

Details on the data processing pipeline are available in Perez-Lamarque et al. (2022). Briefly, [SOURCE: Page_195]

operational taxonomic units (OTUs) were clustered at 97% similarity after merging forward and reverse [SOURCE: Page_195]

sequences and removing chimeras. Negative controls incorporated at the DNA extraction and PCR [SOURCE: Page_195]

steps were used to remove contamination using the decontam v.1.22.0 R package (Davis et al. 2018). [SOURCE: Page_195]

Short and nonfungal sequences were filtered out of the OTU tables. Taxonomic assignments were [SOURCE: Page_195]

performed using the Silva 138.1 database (Quast et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_195]

117 [SOURCE: Page_195]

2.5 Statistical analysis [SOURCE: Page_196]

All analyses, graphs and calculations were performed in R v.4.3.0 (R Core Team, 2024). [SOURCE: Page_196]

To assess differences between soil water systems, the pit and diversity datasets were combined to [SOURCE: Page_196]

maximize the number of samples and sampling areas. The impact of the sampling depth on the [SOURCE: Page_196]

microbial community was assessed only on the basis of pit samples (because the diversity samples [SOURCE: Page_196]

were obtained at a fixed depth of 40 cm with a different sampling protocol). [SOURCE: Page_196]

The alpha diversity of the metabarcoding data was quantified with Hill numbers (Alberdi and Gilbert [SOURCE: Page_196]

2019a) at orders of diversity q = 0 (species richness), q = 1 (exponential of Shannon index) and q = 2 [SOURCE: Page_196]

(inverse of Simpson index), using the hilldiv package v.1.5.1 (Alberdi and Gilbert 2019b). Samples with [SOURCE: Page_196]

< 1,000 reads and OTUs with < 10 reads were discarded to compare differences in alpha diversity [SOURCE: Page_196]

among soil water systems and sampling depths. Differences in diversity among conditions were first [SOURCE: Page_196]

assessed on the rarefied data by analysis of variance (ANOVA). Post-hoc Tukey HSD tests were then [SOURCE: Page_196]

applied to conduct pairwise comparisons between groups. [SOURCE: Page_196]

We then analyzed the beta diversity of fungal compositions among depths or soil water systems. [SOURCE: Page_196]

Samples with < 1,000 reads and OTUs with < 10 reads were removed. All OTU count tables were [SOURCE: Page_196]

Hellinger transformed. Differences in fungal compositions were highlighted using non-metric [SOURCE: Page_196]

multidimensional scaling (NMDS) performed on the Bray-Curtis dissimilarity matrix. The influences of [SOURCE: Page_196]

our factors of interest (soil water systems and sampling depth) and their combined effect were [SOURCE: Page_196]

assessed through permutational analysis of variance (PERMANOVA) with the adonis^2 function in the [SOURCE: Page_196]

vegan package v.2.6-4 (Oksanen et al. 2022). Each soil water systems or sampling depth combination [SOURCE: Page_196]

was then compared two-by-two through multivariate analysis of variance (MANOVA). [SOURCE: Page_196]

Differences in soil parameters between soil water systems were highlighted via principal component [SOURCE: Page_196]

analysis (PCA) and assessed by ANOVA. The effects of the soil parameters and the 10 most significant [SOURCE: Page_196]

taxa of class, order and family taxonomic levels on the fungal composition were assessed by distance- [SOURCE: Page_196]

based redundancy analysis (dbRDA) using the microeco v.1.4.0 package (Liu et al. 2021) and the fungal [SOURCE: Page_196]

Bray-Curtis dissimilarity matrix. The influence of each soil parameter was tested via PERMANOVA. [SOURCE: Page_196]

118 [SOURCE: Page_196]

Differential abundance analyses (DAA) were carried out on the pit sample results from the flooding [SOURCE: Page_197]

irrigation and drip irrigation systems to investigate the impact of soil water systems on taxon [SOURCE: Page_197]

abundances in the irrigation systems. DAA were performed with the DESeq function of the DESeq^2 [SOURCE: Page_197]

package v. 1.40.2 (Love et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_197]

Trophic modes of the most abundant fungal genus in each group (> 5% of total relative abundance) [SOURCE: Page_197]

were retrieved using FUNGuild v.0.2.0.9000 (Nguyen et al. 2016). [SOURCE: Page_197]

2.6 Root architecture parameter measurements [SOURCE: Page_197]

Photos of the soil horizons within the quadrat were examined to measure the number of roots and [SOURCE: Page_197]

mean root width in 10 cm deep layers from 10 cm to 160 cm deep in each of the eight pits. Roots were [SOURCE: Page_197]

counted in each pit at each 10 cm deep bracket. For the root width, the images were analyzed using [SOURCE: Page_197]

ImageJ 1.53K software. The root width (diameter) was measured with the line tool. The root widths in [SOURCE: Page_197]

all layers in each pit were then averaged. Individual roots were classified in four orders according to [SOURCE: Page_197]

their width and on the basis of the classification described by Tahri (2018): order 1 > 1.06 cm; order 2 [SOURCE: Page_197]

> 0.72 cm; order 3 > 0.37 cm and order 4 < 0.37 cm. Hierarchical clustering based on the root order [SOURCE: Page_197]

ratio was applied to check for root order organization consistency between soil water systems. [SOURCE: Page_197]

3 Results [SOURCE: Page_197]

3.1 Effects of sampling depth on the diversity and composition of [SOURCE: Page_197]

fungal communities in date palm roots [SOURCE: Page_197]

The root fungal diversity and community composition in the pit dataset were compared at each [SOURCE: Page_197]

sampling depth. Although the ANOVA and Tukey test revealed no significant differences in alpha [SOURCE: Page_197]

diversity between root depths for species richness (q = 0), the diversity tended to diminish as the [SOURCE: Page_197]

sampling depth increased (Fig. 3a). Although this trend was not significantly confirmed (p > 0.05) at [SOURCE: Page_197]

119 [SOURCE: Page_197]

higher q values when giving more importance to abundant species, differences between the low depth [SOURCE: Page_198]

(40 cm) and high depth (140 cm) showed p values < 0.1. The medium depth samples (80 cm) did not [SOURCE: Page_198]

differ from those of the two other depths, i.e. the depth continuously decreased the alpha diversity. [SOURCE: Page_198]

Although the diversity slightly decreased with depth, the fungal community composition was not [SOURCE: Page_198]

impacted by the sampling depth, as revealed by the PERMANOVA pit sample results (R² = 0.035; p = [SOURCE: Page_198]

0.808) and MANOVA findings between depths (R² of 0.020, 0.029 and 0.030; p = 0.898, 0.515 and 0.706 [SOURCE: Page_198]

for comparisons between low/medium, low/high and medium/high depths respectively) (Fig. 3b). [SOURCE: Page_198]

3.2 Effects of soil water systems on the diversity and composition of [SOURCE: Page_198]

fungal communities in date palm roots [SOURCE: Page_198]

The fungal community diversity and composition in date palm roots in the pit and diversity datasets [SOURCE: Page_198]

were compared between the soil water systems. The ANOVA and Tukey tests on Hill numbers revealed [SOURCE: Page_198]

no significant differences overall in species richness (q = 0) between soil water systems except for the [SOURCE: Page_198]

date palms in abandoned farms which had higher alpha diversity. Drip irrigated date palms had the [SOURCE: Page_198]

lowest diversity at every q value. When rare species were given less weight (q = 1) drip showed [SOURCE: Page_198]

significantly lower diversity, but no significant difference was found at q = 2. Our results showed a [SOURCE: Page_198]

significant effect of soil water system on fungal community structure as revealed by NMDS (Fig. 3d) [SOURCE: Page_198]

and assessed by PERMANOVA on the pit samples (R² = 0.244; p = 9.999e-5) and all samples (pit + [SOURCE: Page_198]

diversity) (R² = 0.177; p = 9.999e-5). In the pit samples, no significant effects of the combination of soil [SOURCE: Page_198]

water system and sampling depth were observed (PERMANOVA; R² = 0.119; p = 0.359). All soil water [SOURCE: Page_198]

system had significantly different fungal communities. The least difference noted was between [SOURCE: Page_198]

flooding and drip irrigation (hereafter referred to as ‘flooding’ and ‘drip’) (MANOVA; R² = 0.071; p = [SOURCE: Page_198]

0.027), while the greatest difference was between drip and abandoned (hereafter referred to as [SOURCE: Page_198]

‘abandoned’) (MANOVA; R² = 0.168; p = 0.001). [SOURCE: Page_198]

120 [SOURCE: Page_198]

The main observed taxa (genera with > 5% total relative abundance) (Table S$_1$) differed among the soil [SOURCE: Page_199]

water groups. Flooding, drip and natural environments (hereafter referred to as ‘natural’) groups [SOURCE: Page_199]

showed high percentages of species of AMF genera among their most represented taxa. Flooding and [SOURCE: Page_199]

drip showed high percentages of Rhizophagus species (20.3 and 31.2% of total reads respectively), [SOURCE: Page_199]

while this genus represented only 5.6% in the natural samples. Drip and natural samples also showed [SOURCE: Page_199]

high percentages of Glomus species (22.5% and 9.24%, respectively), while the natural samples also [SOURCE: Page_199]

had high percentages of species of two other AMF genera, i.e. Sclerocystis and Septoglomus (8.6 and [SOURCE: Page_199]

5.6%). Palms growing on abandoned farms only had one AMF genus, i.e. Funneliformis (11.1%), among [SOURCE: Page_199]

its major taxa. All groups included high extents of saprotrophic fungi (according to FUNGuild) (Table [SOURCE: Page_199]

S$_1$), including: Kraurogymnocarpa (20.7% in flooding and 7.8% in abandoned), Calvatia (16.7% in [SOURCE: Page_199]

flooding and 10.6% in drip), Pseudocolus (8.0% in drip), Ophiosphaerella (31.6% in natural and 18.6% [SOURCE: Page_199]

in abandoned) and Trichocladium (14.9% in abandoned). There were differences in pathotrophic fungi [SOURCE: Page_199]

present in the different soil water systems. Among the main genera, only one pathotroph was noted [SOURCE: Page_199]

in the flooding conditions, i.e. Burgoa (5.09%). This genus was also frequently detected in drip (11.1%) [SOURCE: Page_199]

and natural (5.6%) conditions. Among the other frequently observed pathotrophic fungi, Melampsora [SOURCE: Page_199]

was found in drip (8.6%) and in natural (6.24%), Nowakovskiella was detected in natural (7.6%), [SOURCE: Page_199]

Exophiala and Scopulariopsis (11.6 and 6.2% respectively) in abandoned conditions. [SOURCE: Page_199]

121 [SOURCE: Page_199]

3.3 Soil chemical differences between soil water sample groups [SOURCE: Page_200]

The geochemical parameters of the soils from each soil water group sample are presented in Table 1, [SOURCE: Page_200]

as assessed by principal component analysis (PCA) in Fig. S$_1$. [SOURCE: Page_200]

With pH values > 8 on average, the soils in which the date palms were growing were all alkaline (Table [SOURCE: Page_200]

1). The pHH20 values of soils exposed to drip irrigation (pHH20 8.04 ± 0.54) were significantly lower than [SOURCE: Page_200]

122 [SOURCE: Page_200]

those noted for the three other conditions (pHH20 8.50 on average). The pHKCl values were similar in all [SOURCE: Page_201]

soil water systems. The acidification potential differed significantly between natural (-0.44 ± 0.42) and [SOURCE: Page_201]

drip systems (0.18 ± 0.30). Regarding the atomic elements, the soil silicon contents were high and [SOURCE: Page_201]

ranged from 69% to 81%, and this trend was similar among the four soil water systems. The response [SOURCE: Page_201]

of the soil elements to the soil water systems depended on the atomic elements analyzed. The levels [SOURCE: Page_201]

of atomic elements such as K and Fe in the soil were similar under all of the soil water systems, while [SOURCE: Page_201]

Al, Ca, and Mg were the highest in soils under abandoned farms. Levels of other elements such as Mn [SOURCE: Page_201]

and S were lowest in natural environments. [SOURCE: Page_201]

3.4 Contribution of soil parameters and major taxa to the fungal [SOURCE: Page_202]

community composition [SOURCE: Page_202]

The effects of soil parameters on the fungal communities (Bray-Curtis dissimilarity matrix) were tested [SOURCE: Page_202]

by PERMANOVA and graphically displayed using dbRDAs with ellipses, colored in relation to the soil [SOURCE: Page_202]

water systems (Fig. 4a). Except for Si and pH, all atomic elements analyzed (Mg, Ca, S, K, Fe, Al, Mn and [SOURCE: Page_202]

P) had a significant impact (at p < 0.05) on the fungal community composition in the different soil water [SOURCE: Page_202]

systems. The contributions of major taxa to each soil water system (limited to five to not hinder the [SOURCE: Page_202]

figures readability) at different taxonomic levels were also assessed (Fig. 4 b, c, and d). The top three [SOURCE: Page_202]

taxon classes, orders and families that contributed most to explaining the community compositions [SOURCE: Page_202]

respectively belonged to: (i) Glomeromycetes, Eurothiomycetes and Dothideomycetes classes (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_202]

4b), (ii) Glomerales, Pleosporales and Onygenales orders (Fig. 4c), and (iii) Glomeraceae, [SOURCE: Page_202]

Phaeospheriaceae and Gymnoascaceae families (Fig. 4d). [SOURCE: Page_202]

3.5 Flooding versus drip irrigation systems: differential abundances of [SOURCE: Page_203]

fungal communities [SOURCE: Page_203]

We measured the potential impacts of agricultural practices in active date palm orchards on fungal [SOURCE: Page_203]

communities by focusing on and comparing two watering systems: flooding versus drip irrigation. In [SOURCE: Page_203]

125 [SOURCE: Page_203]

these two farms, the date palm ages were similar and the trees were closely located geographically [SOURCE: Page_204]

(within ≈ 200 m). Moreover, the soil parameters measured in both systems were similar (except for [SOURCE: Page_204]

pH, Mg and Al; Table S$_2$). Differential abundance analyses (DAA) were conducted to identify taxa that [SOURCE: Page_204]

were differentially abundant between the two irrigation conditions (Fig. 5). For drip irrigation, the most [SOURCE: Page_204]

significantly abundant taxa included several OTUs of Glomeraceae, one Melampsoraceae, one [SOURCE: Page_204]

Lycoperdaceae and one Gymnoascaceae (Fig. 5a). Regarding the flooding conditions, DAA highlighted [SOURCE: Page_204]

three OTUs of Eurotiomycetes (two Gymnoascaceae and one Mycocaliciaceae families), two [SOURCE: Page_204]

Dothideomycetes from Cladosporiaceae and Phaeosphaeriaceae families, one Sordariomycetes [SOURCE: Page_204]

(Chaetomiaceae), one Agaricomycetes, one Glomeraceae and one Tremellomycetes (Filobasidiaceae). [SOURCE: Page_204]

3.6 Impact of soil water systems on AMF communities [SOURCE: Page_205]

High proportions of Glomeromycetes (AMF fungi) were detected in the samples and found to be [SOURCE: Page_205]

differentially expressed in relation to the soil water systems. We therefore focused more closely on [SOURCE: Page_205]

these Glomeromycetes. [SOURCE: Page_205]

The proportion of AMF was highest among the drip sampling group, where 44.3% of the total reads [SOURCE: Page_205]

concerned AMF, 34.9% natural samples, 21.4% flooding and 14.5% abandoned farms. A large majority [SOURCE: Page_205]

of the AMF reads belonged to the Glomeromycetes class (> 95%; 46/49 OTU), including a dominance [SOURCE: Page_205]

of the Glomerales order (96.9%), followed by Archaeosporales (1.0%) and Diversisporales (0.7%), (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_205]

6a). Most of the assigned AMF reads belonged to the Glomeraceae family. Apart from Glomeraceae: [SOURCE: Page_205]

(i) two AMF OTUs identified as Ambispora granatensis (Ambisporaceae) were detected in the [SOURCE: Page_205]

abandoned (1.51% of AMF reads) and natural (0.50% of AMF reads) root samples, (ii) one OTU [SOURCE: Page_205]

identified as Redeckera fulvum (Diversisporaceae) was detected in the root samples exposed to [SOURCE: Page_205]

flooding (0.50%) and drip irrigation (0.57%), and (iii) one AMF OTU identified as Racocetra alborosea [SOURCE: Page_205]

(Gigasporaceae) was extracted from root samples exposed to drip irrigation (1.0%) (Fig. 6b). The [SOURCE: Page_205]

proportion of unassigned AMF families differed between soil water systems: 54.8% for drip; 48.2% for [SOURCE: Page_205]

flooding, 41.1% for natural; 6.3% for abandoned (Fig. 6b). [SOURCE: Page_205]

3.7 Date palm root architecture under different soil water systems [SOURCE: Page_206]

After analyzing the environmental DNA, we directly monitored the date palm roots in relation to our [SOURCE: Page_206]

four target soil water systems. Root numbers and root widths were quantified in 10 cm layers within [SOURCE: Page_206]

all pits (Fig. 1c and 1d), and the results are presented in Fig. 7. The date palm root numbers were [SOURCE: Page_206]

heterogenous (Fig. 7a) in terms of depth and soil water systems, where: (i) date palms exposed to flood [SOURCE: Page_206]

and drip irrigation showed similar root number profiles, with a clear increase around 60-70 cm deep, [SOURCE: Page_206]

with the lowest number of roots noted before and after the 60-70 cm depth ; (ii) date palms growing [SOURCE: Page_206]

in natural conditions showed a low number of roots (0 to 25) at all depths; (iii) date palms growing in [SOURCE: Page_206]

abandoned farms (pits N° 7 and 8) had high numbers of roots close to the surface, and then the [SOURCE: Page_206]

numbers decreased at a heterogenous rate with depth, i.e. the root number in pit N°7 decreased at a [SOURCE: Page_206]

faster rate (from 75 to 20 roots in the first 30 cm) than those in pit N°8 (with 50 roots noted in the first [SOURCE: Page_206]

100 cm). Almost no roots were observed at the lowest depth (150-160 cm), except under date palms [SOURCE: Page_206]

exposed to drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_206]

The patterns noted regarding the mean root width in relation to the soil water systems (Fig. 7b) [SOURCE: Page_206]

differed from the root number patterns. Flooded and abandoned date palms had relatively low root [SOURCE: Page_206]

diameters, which slightly increased with depth. The root diameters of date palms under drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_206]

and in natural conditions exhibited heterogenic profiles (Fig. 7b). The hierarchical clustering and [SOURCE: Page_206]

distribution of date palms root orders (based on diameter) revealed a clustering related to the soil [SOURCE: Page_206]

water systems (Fig 7c and 7d). Moreover, the root orders of individual date palms exposed to the same [SOURCE: Page_206]

soil water systems (e.g. two date palms growing in natural conditions) were highly similar (Fig. 7c and [SOURCE: Page_206]

7d). The date palm root samples collected from the abandoned farms had the greatest percentage of [SOURCE: Page_206]

128 [SOURCE: Page_206]

fine roots (order 4 < 0.37 mm Ø) and the lowest percentage of wider roots (order 1 > 1.06 mm Ø). Date [SOURCE: Page_207]

palms exposed to drip irrigation had the greatest proportion of wider roots of order 1 (Fig. 7d). [SOURCE: Page_207]

depth (10 cm layers, from 10 to 160 cm below ground level in 2 m horizons) and colored according to [SOURCE: Page_207]
