in each pit at each depth. b: Mean date palm root width. c: Clustering of pits according to the date [SOURCE: Page_207]

129 [SOURCE: Page_207]

4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_208]

4.1 soil water systems affect the root density and root width [SOURCE: Page_208]

In our study, cultivated date palms irrigated with flooding and drip irrigation systems had a maximum [SOURCE: Page_208]

root density at around 60-70 cm depth, and this density decreased notably after 100 cm. These results [SOURCE: Page_208]

were in line with those of other studies (Munier 1973; Tahri 2018), where the highest densities were [SOURCE: Page_208]

detected in roughly the same horizons. Moreover, no differences were observed between individuals [SOURCE: Page_208]

under flooding and drip irrigation systems. This was inconsistent with our hypothesis and Bourziza et [SOURCE: Page_208]

al. (2017b) who stated that date palms grown under surface drip irrigation developed more roots near [SOURCE: Page_208]

the surface and these roots were thus more susceptible to desiccation. This is an important factor [SOURCE: Page_208]

because flooding irrigation systems consume much higher amounts of water compared to drip [SOURCE: Page_208]

irrigation (Alazba 2004; Amiri et al. 2007). [SOURCE: Page_208]

The two individual date palms investigated in abandoned farms showed a variable high number of [SOURCE: Page_208]

roots at the surface. This variation may be explained by the date palm age, where individual N°8 was [SOURCE: Page_208]

taller and growing on an older farm as compared to individual N°7. Daddi Bouhoun (2010) reported [SOURCE: Page_208]

that the overall root density was not affected by the date palm ages, but that the hydro-edaphic [SOURCE: Page_208]

properties of the soils may have an impact. Moreover, the same author estimated that respiratory [SOURCE: Page_208]

roots (located at shallower depths with respiratory functions) were more numerous in older date palm [SOURCE: Page_208]

trees with higher respiratory needs. In our case, the greater number of roots noted at shallow depths [SOURCE: Page_208]

in farms could actually have been respiratory roots and the number could partly be explained by the [SOURCE: Page_208]

palm tree ages, which were older than those of the flooding and drip irrigation samples. As the samples [SOURCE: Page_208]

were obtained on different farms, the differences in the root density profiles could be attributed to [SOURCE: Page_208]

differences in the hydro-edaphic properties of the soils in the two farms. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Date palms are also frequently found growing in natural conditions. The extent of water availability [SOURCE: Page_208]

(able to influence the root systems) differs between farm and natural conditions (Daddi Bouhoun [SOURCE: Page_208]

2010), i.e. date palms growing in natural conditions are not irrigated and depend exclusively on [SOURCE: Page_208]

130 [SOURCE: Page_208]

precipitation. Hence, it could be assumed that the rooting architecture of date palms in farms differs [SOURCE: Page_209]

from that of trees growing in natural conditions. In our study, the root architecture of uncultivated [SOURCE: Page_209]

date palms hosted in natural desert ecosystems differed greatly from that of trees growing in other [SOURCE: Page_209]

environments. These individuals had a homogenous low root density at all depths, with almost no [SOURCE: Page_209]

roots observed in the 150-160 cm layer. These results strongly suggest that agriculture, and probably [SOURCE: Page_209]

irrigation, significantly modifies plant root systems compared to natural ecosystems. [SOURCE: Page_209]

Comprehensive descriptions of the root architecture of large plants at great depths are currently [SOURCE: Page_209]

lacking. Although date palm roots have been reported to be present at depths > 10 m (Zaid and de Wet [SOURCE: Page_209]

2002), there is no evidence to support this. However, deep roots (> 20 m) have been described in [SOURCE: Page_209]

Sideroxylon lanuginosum and Quercus fusiformis trees growing in a semiarid environment (Johnson et [SOURCE: Page_209]

al. 2014). This rooting architecture and strategy may provide access to deep nutrients and water [SOURCE: Page_209]

(McCulley et al. 2004). Soil water systems strongly affects date palms root formation. In these natural [SOURCE: Page_209]

environments where water is scarce, uncultivated date palms might have developed a higher density [SOURCE: Page_209]

of roots in deeper layers to get water and nutrients absent from the surface. Note also that we [SOURCE: Page_209]

monitored the roots in a flat area 2 m away from the trunk. The root system is a 3D object described [SOURCE: Page_209]

here with a 2D profile, so the monitored root parameters may vary with the distance from the trunk. [SOURCE: Page_209]

In deserts soils, nutrients can be heterogeneously distributed horizontally (Schlesinger et al. 1996; [SOURCE: Page_209]

Maurice et al. 2023) and vertically (Eilers et al. 2012). Notably, nutrients and organic matter are mainly [SOURCE: Page_209]

concentrated in the topsoil layers where most nutrient cycling occurs (Jobbágy and Jackson 2001; Eilers [SOURCE: Page_209]

et al. 2012). Plant root systems often adapt to the extent of water and nutrient availability in soils [SOURCE: Page_209]

(Cordeiro et al. 2020), whereas plants growing in deserts also develop adaptations to help them get [SOURCE: Page_209]

established and survive in these dry and hot environments. For example, Stipagrostis spp. develop a [SOURCE: Page_209]

rhizosheath, while accumulating fine roots that host various microorganisms. This microbiome can [SOURCE: Page_209]

increase the soil moisture, modify the pH and nutrient availability, thus helping the plant to thrive in [SOURCE: Page_209]

these extreme environments (Tian et al. 2019). Date palm seedlings have also been found to develop [SOURCE: Page_209]

adaptations to arid conditions, with a multilayered tube organ called the cotyledonary petiole, which [SOURCE: Page_209]

131 [SOURCE: Page_209]

enables so-called remote germination in deeper soils to escape the high surface temperatures (Xiao et [SOURCE: Page_210]

al. 2019). [SOURCE: Page_210]

Overall, very few studies have investigated the root parameters of adult date palms due to the [SOURCE: Page_210]

difficulty of monitoring and measuring the root system of large plants. All studies that have attempted [SOURCE: Page_210]

to describe root parameters in date palms were focused on agrosystems or on irrigated farming [SOURCE: Page_210]

conditions (Daddi Bouhoun 2010; Littardi et al. 2015; Tahri 2018). From this standpoint, our study [SOURCE: Page_210]

generated new elements and knowledge on the rooting architecture of date palms in natural [SOURCE: Page_210]

conditions. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Munier (1973) reported that most date palm roots were present in the top 100 cm soil horizon. [SOURCE: Page_210]

However, the author did not mention the method used in the book to investigate date palm root [SOURCE: Page_210]

systems, yet his claim is often considered as a baseline (Zaid and de Wet 2002; Tahri 2018). Otherwise, [SOURCE: Page_210]

Tahri (2018) measured the root density (in kg of roots/m^3 of soil) and root system diameter under three [SOURCE: Page_210]

date palms in 100 cm deep pits, while Daddi Bouhoun (2010) used an auger and thereby assessed the [SOURCE: Page_210]

root density and root diameter in 120 cm soil profiles. To the best of our knowledge, no studies have [SOURCE: Page_210]

tried to explore the date palm root architecture below 120 cm. Here we thus collected valuable data [SOURCE: Page_210]

on the date palm root architecture up to 160 cm soil depth. [SOURCE: Page_210]

4.2 Fungal community diversity slightly declines with the soil depth [SOURCE: Page_210]

without changes in the community composition [SOURCE: Page_210]

Here we sequenced date palm root fungal communities. These communities were composed of fungi [SOURCE: Page_210]

detected within the roots (endophytes) and on their surface (rhizoplane), although the latter [SOURCE: Page_210]

communities might have been reduced as the roots were rinsed before analysis. Our results revealed [SOURCE: Page_210]

that fungal diversity in date palm roots slightly decreased along a depth gradient. This trend was in [SOURCE: Page_210]

accordance with that observed previously in studies on soil microbial communities (Eilers et al. 2012; [SOURCE: Page_210]

Hao et al. 2021; He et al. 2023b). The decreasing diversity pattern we observed could have been a [SOURCE: Page_210]

132 [SOURCE: Page_210]

direct consequence of a decrease in fungal diversity in the soils, thereby reducing the fungal pool [SOURCE: Page_211]

available for horizontal soil-to-root transmission. Laurent-Webb et al. (2023) found that horizontal [SOURCE: Page_211]

transmission from soil to roots was a major factor explaining fungal community composition in the [SOURCE: Page_211]

roots of adults Amaranthaceae Haloxylon salicornicum. [SOURCE: Page_211]

Most current knowledge on soil microbial communities derives from studies on surface soils, which [SOURCE: Page_211]

tend to have higher nutrient proportions and greater microbial diversity (Hao et al. 2021) than deeper [SOURCE: Page_211]

soils. Fewer studies have focused on deep soil microbial communities and hence these communities [SOURCE: Page_211]

are less understood (He et al. 2023b). Below the topsoil layer, the soil pH tends to increase with depth, [SOURCE: Page_211]

while carbon, nitrogen and organic matter contents generally decrease along a depth gradient. In their [SOURCE: Page_211]

meta-analysis, He et al. (2023b) observed that the bacterial biomass and diversity also decreased with [SOURCE: Page_211]

soil depth in different environments. Geochemical properties and abiotic factors (e.g. soil pressure, [SOURCE: Page_211]

reduced oxygen levels, reduced nitrogen quantity) may serve as a strong microbial community filter [SOURCE: Page_211]

(Chen et al. 2016). Furthermore, plant roots select their associated microbial communities from the [SOURCE: Page_211]

soil (Dang et al. 2021). Microbes may associate with plant roots in three ways: (i) horizontal [SOURCE: Page_211]

transmission by direct penetration of microbes from the surrounding soil into the roots, (ii) transfer [SOURCE: Page_211]

from older roots while growing, and (iii) vertical transmission of endophytes from seeds (Laurent- [SOURCE: Page_211]

Webb et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_211]

The fungal diversity decreased with soil depth whereas the fungal community compositions were not [SOURCE: Page_211]

significantly affected. However, many studies have reported the presence of significantly different [SOURCE: Page_211]

microbial communities in the topsoil layer (with high nutrient contents) compared to deeper levels [SOURCE: Page_211]

(Eilers et al. 2012; Gu et al. 2017; Schlatter et al. 2018; He et al. 2023a). We began our sampling at 40 [SOURCE: Page_211]

cm depth (much below the topsoil layer), thereby potentially excluding the influence of the topsoil [SOURCE: Page_211]

microbiome. Desert soils are also less stratified and more homogeneous than those in forests or [SOURCE: Page_211]

cropfields described in other studies, which could explain the absence of differences we noted [SOURCE: Page_211]

between our sampling depths. Finally, our study focused on root fungal communities while other [SOURCE: Page_211]

previous studies concerned soil and rhizosphere samples. [SOURCE: Page_211]

133 [SOURCE: Page_211]

In our study, the deepest samples were collected in the 160 cm soil layer. Although the soils were [SOURCE: Page_212]

found to be mostly homogeneous along a vertical gradient, interesting features may appear in horizons [SOURCE: Page_212]

below 160 cm. For example, in a study carried out in the hyperarid Atacama Desert in Chile, Horstmann [SOURCE: Page_212]

et al. (2024) reported that the soil was composed of two major geological strata separated at 2 m [SOURCE: Page_212]

depth. The lower gypsum stratum showed higher bacterial diversity, probably due to higher water [SOURCE: Page_212]

availability in this horizon. The same structure might also prevail in AlUla soils, so the microbial [SOURCE: Page_212]

communities present in deeper soil layers might differ. [SOURCE: Page_212]

We described a reduction in fungal diversity in deeper soils, but we also found that there were no [SOURCE: Page_212]

community composition differences between soil depths. Wang et al. (2021) reported that microbiome [SOURCE: Page_212]

multifunctionality decreased along a soil depth gradient (0-100 cm). It would be interesting to further [SOURCE: Page_212]

analyze these effects to determine if the microbiome functional properties are conserved along a [SOURCE: Page_212]

depth gradient. [SOURCE: Page_212]

4.3 soil water systems affect date palm root fungal communities [SOURCE: Page_212]

4.3.1 Soil water systems impact the diversity and composition of date palm [SOURCE: Page_212]

root fungal communities [SOURCE: Page_212]

In our study, only drip irrigation had an effect on fungal diversity, which was lower than noted in the [SOURCE: Page_212]

other soil water systems at diversity orders q = 1 and q = 2, which was out of line with our hypothesis [SOURCE: Page_212]

that variations would only be detected between date palms growing in irrigated and non-irrigated [SOURCE: Page_212]

conditions. As the drip irrigation systems had only been installed a few years prior to the study, this [SOURCE: Page_212]

difference in drip fungal diversity could be attributed to a short-term disturbance effect due to the [SOURCE: Page_212]

replacement of the irrigation system, but this effect might disappear within a few years once the [SOURCE: Page_212]

system is stabilized. Otherwise, Wang et al. (2023b) reported a continuous reduction in soil fungal [SOURCE: Page_212]

diversity 11 to 21 years after the application of mulched drip irrigation in cotton crop fields, indicating [SOURCE: Page_212]

that changes in irrigation systems can trigger long-term changes in the fungal communities. In an arid [SOURCE: Page_212]

134 [SOURCE: Page_212]

region, Deng et al. (2022) observed lower fungal diversity in drip irrigated rhizosphere soil compared [SOURCE: Page_213]

to flooding irrigated rhizosphere soil under alfalfa crops. Abumaali et al. (2023) reported lower [SOURCE: Page_213]

bacterial diversity in the rhizosphere of natural date palms under non-irrigated conditions than in the [SOURCE: Page_213]

rhizosphere of cultivated date palms, in contrast to our results as we did not observe any diversity [SOURCE: Page_213]

differences when comparing flooded and natural date palm fungal communities. Abumaali et al. (2023) [SOURCE: Page_213]

explained this difference by the different soil physicochemical properties, yet we noted high [SOURCE: Page_213]

intracondition (soil water systems) variability but no significant differences between conditions. [SOURCE: Page_213]

Compared with flooding irrigation, drip irrigation modifies the wetting and drying cycles, water [SOURCE: Page_213]

distribution and chemical properties of soils (Bourziza et al. 2017b; Alnaim et al. 2022). Flooding [SOURCE: Page_213]

irrigation induces regular flushing events, leaching of upper layer nutrients and salt to deeper soil [SOURCE: Page_213]

layers, alongside intense short wetting events followed by drying periods. Drip irrigation reduces [SOURCE: Page_213]

drainage, thus leading to more stable soil moisture conditions for the plants and microbial [SOURCE: Page_213]

communities. However, surface drip irrigation induces salt accumulation on the soil surface due to the [SOURCE: Page_213]

absence of leaching. The water application period with drip irrigation systems is longer and the [SOURCE: Page_213]

watering is mainly concentrated in the upper soil layers, so the soil moisture levels in the deep soil [SOURCE: Page_213]

layers might be less than under flooding irrigation. Although water application is more consistent with [SOURCE: Page_213]

drip irrigation systems, a change in irrigation system on farms may substantially modify the water [SOURCE: Page_213]

distribution and flow. This disturbance might potentially dry the deep soil layers due to the lack of [SOURCE: Page_213]

leaching at the surface following irrigation, leading to lower water penetration to these depths. The [SOURCE: Page_213]

abandoned farms we investigated had also been disturbed—although long before the study—by an [SOURCE: Page_213]

interruption in irrigation, thereby forcing the date palms and associated communities to adapt and [SOURCE: Page_213]

restructure in response to the new scant water conditions. Otherwise, from the moment of their [SOURCE: Page_213]

germination, the date palms studied in the natural environment were growing in stable conditions with [SOURCE: Page_213]

limited water access and scarce rainfall (≈ 0, 1 or 2 events/year). [SOURCE: Page_213]

The fungal community composition significantly differed among all soil water systems that we studied. [SOURCE: Page_213]

This pattern has also been frequently observed in other studies where changes in irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_213]

135 [SOURCE: Page_213]

(Deng et al. 2022; Wang et al. 2023a), water quality (Lüneberg et al. 2019), and water quantity (Wang [SOURCE: Page_214]

et al. 2017) altered the microbial communities in soils and roots. However, the majority of these [SOURCE: Page_214]

studies were focused on soil or rhizosphere microbiota. Here we assessed date palm root (endophyte [SOURCE: Page_214]

and rhizoplane) microbiota, which differ from soil microbiota, due to the filtering effect and specific [SOURCE: Page_214]

niches offered by the roots. [SOURCE: Page_214]

Fungal communities are very dependent on water conditions and nutrients (Schlatter et al. 2018) and [SOURCE: Page_214]

can be affected by disturbance effects such as agricultural practices (Verbruggen and Kiers 2010; Wang [SOURCE: Page_214]

et al. 2017). Roots also seem to select their fungal partners in response to abiotic and biotic stresses [SOURCE: Page_214]

such as hydro-edaphic conditions through changes in root exudation and in their immune system [SOURCE: Page_214]

(Rolfe et al. 2019; Deng et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_214]

4.3.2 Soil water systems alter the fungal taxa composition in date palm roots [SOURCE: Page_214]

Among the major fungal taxa detected in date palm roots under all soil water systems (> 5% relative [SOURCE: Page_214]

abundance), most were saprotrophic according to FUNGuild v.0.2.0.9000 (Nguyen et al. 2016) [SOURCE: Page_214]

(Kraurogymnocarpa, Calvatia, Pseudocolus, Ophiospaerella, Trichocladium). Saprotrophs are [SOURCE: Page_214]

decomposer fungi that play a key role in ecosystems by recycling organic matter (Boddy and Hiscox [SOURCE: Page_214]

2016). Their abundant presence in roots under all soil water systems and the high number of dead [SOURCE: Page_214]

roots observed during our sampling (mostly under non-irrigated date palms) suggests potentially high [SOURCE: Page_214]

organic matter recycling activity. Date palm saprotrophs may therefore contribute to dynamic [SOURCE: Page_214]

reshaping and growth of fresh plant roots by providing ready-to-use nutrients extracted from older [SOURCE: Page_214]

roots (Boddy and Hiscox 2016; Zhang et al. 2021). [SOURCE: Page_214]

The pathotrophic fungal communities associated with date palm roots differed under the different soil [SOURCE: Page_214]

water systems. Among the major taxa, date palms under flooding irrigation hosted one pathotroph [SOURCE: Page_214]

taxon, i.e. Burgoa, which is considered to be a lichen parasite (Lawrey and Diederich 2003). More [SOURCE: Page_214]

abundant plant pathogenic fungi were observed in the other soil water systems, e.g. Rhizoctonia and [SOURCE: Page_214]

Cryphonectria. Pathotrophs are key ecosystem elements that occupy ecological niches and compete [SOURCE: Page_214]

136 [SOURCE: Page_214]

with potential new invasive pathogens, and their presence in roots can thus represent an element of [SOURCE: Page_215]

ecological stability and tolerance to new pathogenic disturbances (Seabloom et al. 2015). Hence, date [SOURCE: Page_215]

palms growing in flooding irrigation conditions might be more susceptible to infection by new [SOURCE: Page_215]

pathogens. For example, Fusarium oxysporum f. sp. albedinis, which causes devastating Bayoud [SOURCE: Page_215]

disease in date palm stands in Morocco and Algeria, is not yet present in Saudi Arabia (Saleh et al. [SOURCE: Page_215]

2015). However, this disease is slowly disseminating eastward from North Africa and might become [SOURCE: Page_215]

problematic in Saudi Arabia in the future if phytosanitary procedures are not fulfilled. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Finally, the main symbiotroph fungi identified were almost all arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) of [SOURCE: Page_215]

various genera (seven overall). Date palms are commonly subject to mycorrhizal colonization under [SOURCE: Page_215]

cultivation conditions (Ramoliya and Pandey 2003; Abohatem et al. 2011; Bouamri et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_215]

Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi, and particularly Glomus and Rhizophagus, were among the main genera [SOURCE: Page_215]

explaining fungal community differences in date palm roots in our conditions. Among [SOURCE: Page_215]

Glomeromycetes, in our samples, we almost exclusively noted OTUs belonging to Glomeraceae [SOURCE: Page_215]

(Glomerales). AMF are generally ubiquitous fungi that prevail in most environments, including arid soil. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Glomeraceae is often found to be dominant in soils under adverse conditions as they are opportunistic [SOURCE: Page_215]

and have evolved characteristics enabling their survival in these environments (Lenoir et al. 2016). This [SOURCE: Page_215]

pattern has been observed in other dryland studies (Symanczik et al. 2014; Qiang et al. 2019; Vasar et [SOURCE: Page_215]

al. 2021; Harrower and Gilbert 2021; Adenan et al. 2021), in the date palm rhizosphere (Al-Yahya’ei et [SOURCE: Page_215]

al. 2011), in Coffea arabica roots (Mahdhi et al. 2020), and in maize (Lü et al. 2020). [SOURCE: Page_215]

Compared to other soil water systems, drip irrigation induced a significant reduction in fungal diversity [SOURCE: Page_215]

and an increase in the proportion and differential abundance of Glomeromycetes. These results [SOURCE: Page_215]

contrast with those of Deng et al. (2022), who reported a proportional decrease in AMF in alfalfa crops [SOURCE: Page_215]

under drip irrigation as compared to flooding irrigation. AMF-plant relationships can be influenced by [SOURCE: Page_215]

the plant and fungal partners and by the environmental conditions, e.g. nutrient or water availability [SOURCE: Page_215]

(Werner and Kiers 2015). In our conditions, we observed a reduction in fungal diversity under drip [SOURCE: Page_215]

irrigation and an increase in the relative abundance of AMF. This suggests that the date palms might [SOURCE: Page_215]

137 [SOURCE: Page_215]

have filtered AMF taxa in the roots or in the rhizosphere in response to the change of soil water [SOURCE: Page_216]

systems, thereby preserving AMF from the overall decrease in diversity. [SOURCE: Page_216]

4.4 Prospects for drip irrigation as an alternative to flooding [SOURCE: Page_216]

In this study, we compared the impacts of using flooding irrigation and drip irrigation on date palm [SOURCE: Page_216]

root system parameters and root fungal communities in a modern monoculture farm setting. We [SOURCE: Page_216]

observed no root architecture differences between the two conditions. We found that fungal diversity [SOURCE: Page_216]

was significantly lower in drip irrigated date palm roots but that proportion of AMF was higher under [SOURCE: Page_216]

these conditions. Fungal communities significantly differed between these two irrigation systems. [SOURCE: Page_216]

Extensive literature, mainly on modern monoculture farms, confirms the superiority of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_216]

over other surface irrigation methods such as bubbler, sprinkler or flooding irrigation in terms of plant [SOURCE: Page_216]

growth, yield, disease reduction and water use efficiency in modern date palm cultivation (Carr 2012; [SOURCE: Page_216]

Elfeky and Elfaki 2019). However, no data is currently available on the impacts of these irrigation [SOURCE: Page_216]

systems on microbial communities associated with date palms. In this study of date palm root fungal [SOURCE: Page_216]

communities, we found no evidence that would suggest that replacing flooding irrigation systems by [SOURCE: Page_216]

drip irrigation systems would be deleterious to date palms. Therefore, our findings support the notion [SOURCE: Page_216]

that shifting from flooding irrigation to drip irrigation in monoculture farms could help substantially [SOURCE: Page_216]

reduce water consumption in modern date palm farms. Nonetheless, to the best of our knowledge, [SOURCE: Page_216]

this is one of the few studies that have focused on investigating the impact of soil water systems on [SOURCE: Page_216]

microbial communities, and it is the only one on date palm. Future studies could focus on assessing [SOURCE: Page_216]

the impacts of soil water systems on microbial functions in soils and roots in relation to date palm [SOURCE: Page_216]

fitness. [SOURCE: Page_216]

Surface drip irrigation nevertheless has several flaws. This irrigation method tends to increase soil [SOURCE: Page_216]

moisture levels at shallow depths, leading to greater surface root development, potentially making [SOURCE: Page_216]

them more susceptible to heat, wind and desiccation, while reducing soil anchorage (Bourziza et al. [SOURCE: Page_216]

138 [SOURCE: Page_216]

2017b). Heat and evaporation at the soil surface can also lead to salt concentration on the surface [SOURCE: Page_217]

(that can be mitigated with leaching steps once in a while) and in the irrigation pipes, thereby damaging [SOURCE: Page_217]

and obstructing them. Subsurface drip irrigation, which is an alternative to the conventional surface [SOURCE: Page_217]

drip irrigation studied here, has also been extensively studied and shown to enhance water savings [SOURCE: Page_217]

and yields (Al-Amoud 2010; Talat Farid Ahmed 2012; Bourziza et al. 2017a, b). This system may be an [SOURCE: Page_217]

even better alternative to flooding than surface drip irrigation and should now be studied with regard [SOURCE: Page_217]

to its impact on date palm microbial communities. This system could notably be used at different [SOURCE: Page_217]

depths, i.e. the soil depth would alter the water distribution, as well as root development and water [SOURCE: Page_217]

conductance in the soil (Al-Amoud 2008; Bourziza et al. 2017b). In further studies, it would be [SOURCE: Page_217]

interesting to assess the effects of the installation depth of this system on microbial communities. [SOURCE: Page_217]

Indeed, we did not observe any microbial community differences between soil depths in our conditions [SOURCE: Page_217]

when comparing surface drip irrigation and flooding irrigation, but direct water input at deeper layers [SOURCE: Page_217]

could alter the community compositions in the upper and lower soil layers. [SOURCE: Page_217]

Water-saving strategies and precise irrigation systems such as drip irrigation systems are probably the [SOURCE: Page_217]

best suited for extensive farms and date palm monoculture conditions, but they could be out of line [SOURCE: Page_217]

with traditional or modern agroecological systems where other crops are also grown in date palm [SOURCE: Page_217]

orchards (e.g. fruits, cereals, vegetables), which thus benefit from the excessive watering (carried out [SOURCE: Page_217]

to meet the date palm water needs) (Battesti 2005). There is currently increased interest in [SOURCE: Page_217]

agroecology in arid and oasian environments as a response to climate change to increase and diversify [SOURCE: Page_217]

farm production overall (Ameur et al. 2020; Mrabet 2022). A choice thus has to be made between: (i) [SOURCE: Page_217]

date palm monoculture agricultural models with adapted irrigation systems to save as much water as [SOURCE: Page_217]

possible, and (ii) agroecosystems with less water use efficiency for date production but enabling crop [SOURCE: Page_217]

and production diversification. [SOURCE: Page_217]

139 [SOURCE: Page_217]

5 Conclusion: [SOURCE: Page_218]

The present study generated new insight into the impacts of different irrigated and non-irrigated [SOURCE: Page_218]

systems on the date palm root architecture and root fungal communities. We outlined the effects of [SOURCE: Page_218]

the soil water systems on the date palm root number and root width at various depths, thus providing [SOURCE: Page_218]

valuable insight into the largely unknown root architecture of mature date palms. Our analysis [SOURCE: Page_218]

revealed that the irrigation system had no impact on the observed root architecture parameters, but [SOURCE: Page_218]

that this architecture was affected by the interruption or absence of irrigation. Moreover, we have [SOURCE: Page_218]

highlighted significant impacts of the soil water systems on the fungal community composition and on [SOURCE: Page_218]

the selection of dominant taxa in the roots. We highlighted that the root sampling depth did not affect [SOURCE: Page_218]

the fungal communities, yet the diversity decreased with increasing depth. [SOURCE: Page_218]

This study paves the way for gaining further essential insight into microbial communities associated [SOURCE: Page_218]

with date palms under different irrigation systems in a context of increasing water scarcity. Future [SOURCE: Page_218]

research will be necessary to assess further potential modifications in microbial functional traits [SOURCE: Page_218]

resulting from changes in irrigation systems. Microbial communities associated with plants are key [SOURCE: Page_218]

factors for agriculture sustainability and could improve the water use efficiency in regions threatened [SOURCE: Page_218]

by water scarcity. They should hence be closely studied to facilitate the adaptation of agrosystems to [SOURCE: Page_218]

current global changes. [SOURCE: Page_218]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_232]

Contexte de l’étude Ce troisième et dernier chapitre, vise à explorer plus avant les données de mycorhization du dattier et tenter de comprendre les possibles origines de la rareté de la mycorhize arbusculaire chez ces plantes de culture. En effet, les CMA peuvent représenter de grands potentiels pour l’amélioration de la nutrition hydrique des plantes et donc participer, si elle est comprise et correctement gérée à réduire la consommation en eau du dattier tout en maintenant la production en quantité et en qualité. [SOURCE: Page_232]

Cette étude se base sur l’ensemble des prélèvements de dattiers collectés lors des différentes missions de prélèvement en Octobre-Novembre 2021, Mars et Juin 2022. En tout plus d’une centaine de dattiers collectés à AlUla dans sa diversité de conditions de cultures, de sols, et de localisation. La mycorhize arbusculaire de tous ces échantillons a été mesurée par histologie et microscopie. Ce chapitre permet de parler un peu plus dans le détail des types de fermes tels que caractérisés par le travail de Muriel Gros-Balthazard et Vincent Battesti. Nous essayons de décrire les possibles différences de taux de mycorhize arbusculaire entre ces types d’exploitations et de revenir également aux types d’irrigations. L’effet des paramètres géochimiques du sol sont regardés pour tenter de comprendre quels éléments peuvent favoriser ou réduire les chances de trouver de la mycorhize arbusculaire dans des racines de dattier. [SOURCE: Page_232]

Des palmiers dattiers ont été également prélevés dans la région proche de Khaybar dans deux fermes abandonnées et une ferme active. Ces prélèvements sont utilisés comme élément de comparaison avec la région d’AlUla, pour tenter d’appréhender à quel point cette région est particulière. Nous étudions également les racines de plantes compagnes prélevées au pied des dattiers pour identifier si les sols habités par ces palmiers ont bel et bien un potentiel mycorhizien pour d’autres plantes et caractériser d’autant plus la spécificité du dattier dans cette région. [SOURCE: Page_232]

152 [SOURCE: Page_232]

Chapitre 3: Near absence of arbuscular mycorrhizae in date palms of the hyperarid region of AlUla [SOURCE: Page_234]

Ce projet d’article est préparé en vue de sa soumission au journal Mycorrhiza [SOURCE: Page_234]

154 [SOURCE: Page_236]

Abstract: [SOURCE: Page_237]

The date palm is a key fruit crop in arid regions of North Africa and Southwest Asia. These regions are [SOURCE: Page_237]

subjected to severe water depletion accentuated by climate change endangering agriculture and [SOURCE: Page_237]

overall livelihood. The date palm is dependent on irrigation for maintaining high growth and yields. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Arbuscular mycorrhization is often considered to be a part of the date palm ecosystems and is [SOURCE: Page_237]

supposed to improve nutrition and stress tolerance of this crop. In this study, we analysed the [SOURCE: Page_237]

mycorrhization of >100 date palms after staining in the hyperarid hyperalkaline region of AlUla in Saudi [SOURCE: Page_237]

Arabia. We reveal that mycorrhization of the date palm in AlUla is scarce while roots of companion [SOURCE: Page_237]

plants growing close to this crop are more frequently found mycorrhizal. Mycorrhization in date palms [SOURCE: Page_237]

was more present in farms using new farming practices and almost absent in feral date palms from [SOURCE: Page_237]

natural desert conditions. We also analyse that mycorrhization was reduced with increasing pH, [SOURCE: Page_237]

phosphorus and aluminium in the soils. Mycorrhization of date palm roots in AlUla is thus, [SOURCE: Page_237]

exceptionally low and the region could be interesting for increasing the knowledge over the [SOURCE: Page_237]

mycorrhization of this crop in extreme arid conditions. We interrogate the role and the possible [SOURCE: Page_237]

advantages of arbuscular mycorrhization in these extreme cropping conditions. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Keywords: Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi; Date palm; Desert [SOURCE: Page_237]

155 [SOURCE: Page_237]

1 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_238]

The date palm (Phoenix dactylifera L.) is one of the oldest cultivated fruit crops in the world, with over [SOURCE: Page_238]

5,000 years of cultivation history, and has long been a staple food in the arid regions of North Africa [SOURCE: Page_238]

and Southwest Asia (Chao and Krueger 2007). It is cultivated for its fruit, the date, in regions ranging [SOURCE: Page_238]

from India to southern Europe, and even in Australia, Mexico and the USA. Still, the major date [SOURCE: Page_238]

producing countries (Egypt, Iran, and Saudi Arabia; FAO data, 2021) are located in the historical [SOURCE: Page_238]

cultivation regions of North Africa and Southwest Asia. Nowadays, the date palm can be found in [SOURCE: Page_238]

various farm types and under different irrigation methods across these diverse locations. This culture [SOURCE: Page_238]

can be found in a gradient of man investment practices from no man-investment date palms harvested [SOURCE: Page_238]

by nomad populations, to intensified monovarietal, cash crop monospecific farms with exportation [SOURCE: Page_238]

aims (Battesti 2005; Gros-Balthazard, Battesti et al. 2023). Between these two extremes lies a [SOURCE: Page_238]

continuum of practices with the traditional date palm orchards showcasing crop species and cultivar [SOURCE: Page_238]

diversity within two or three cultivation strata. These orchards utilize the favorable, more humid and [SOURCE: Page_238]

shadier condition offered by date palm canopies to grow fruit trees and/or cereals and vegetables (de [SOURCE: Page_238]

Grenade 2013). [SOURCE: Page_238]

Date palm is dependent on irrigation water to maintain high yields, growth rate, and fruit quality (Chao [SOURCE: Page_238]

and Krueger 2007). The traditional method for irrigating date palms is the flooding method, which [SOURCE: Page_238]

involves delivering large amounts of water to basins around the base of the date palms. However, this [SOURCE: Page_238]

method is very water-intensive, and much of the water is not absorbed by the date palms. While [SOURCE: Page_238]

overirrigation might benefit stratified date palm orchards by providing excess water to other crops, it [SOURCE: Page_238]

results in significant water losses, especially in monoculture farms, where all the water is supposed to [SOURCE: Page_238]

be directed to the date palm. [SOURCE: Page_238]

Arid regions face water shortages as the water used for irrigation often comes from underground non- [SOURCE: Page_238]

renewable stocks which are depleting rapidly due to overuse (Seraphin et al. 2022; Odnoletkova and [SOURCE: Page_238]

Patzek 2023). This water shortage also increases the salinity of irrigation water and soils (Naorem et [SOURCE: Page_238]

al. 2023). The adaptation of irrigation system to less consuming systems, such as drip irrigation, is one [SOURCE: Page_238]

156 [SOURCE: Page_238]

of the solutions provided to reduce the date palm cultivation water losses (Carr 2012; Elfeky and Elfaki [SOURCE: Page_239]

2019). [SOURCE: Page_239]

Other solutions could be linked to the microbial communities of date palms such as plant growth [SOURCE: Page_239]

promoting rhizobacteria (Anli et al. 2020a; Tahiri et al. 2021; Ou-Zine et al. 2023a) but also arbuscular [SOURCE: Page_239]

mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) to improve its growth and water absorbing capacities (Ait-El-Mokhtar et al. [SOURCE: Page_239]

2019, 2021; Anli et al. 2020b). AMF form the arbuscular mycorrhizae, a symbiotic association with the [SOURCE: Page_239]

roots of ~80% of vascular plant species (Brundrett and Tedersoo 2018). In this symbiosis, the AMF, [SOURCE: Page_239]

pertaining to the Glomeromycotina subphylum (Spatafora et al. 2017), enters the cortical cells of the [SOURCE: Page_239]

roots and forms characteristic structures, arbuscules within cells, hyphae, vesicles, spores, that enables [SOURCE: Page_239]

an exchange of nutrients and various metabolites between the fungus and the host plant. Typically, [SOURCE: Page_239]

the fungi develop thin extraradical hyphae, improving the surface exchange with the soil and absorb [SOURCE: Page_239]

nutrients and water to share with the plant. In return the plant offers lipids and carbohydrates issued [SOURCE: Page_239]

from photosynthesis (van der Heijden et al. 2015). Arbuscular mycorrhizae offer several benefits to the [SOURCE: Page_239]

plants in the arid conditions, remarkably by absorbing the hardly accessible water and nutrients in [SOURCE: Page_239]

these dry, nutrient scarce soils (Apple 2010) and by improving abiotic and biotic stresses tolerance [SOURCE: Page_239]

(Jaiti et al. 2007). Date palm has been observed several times to benefit from arbuscular mycorrhizae [SOURCE: Page_239]

(Al-Karaki 2013). Several studies evidenced spores of AMF in the rhizosphere of date palms, e.g. in [SOURCE: Page_239]

Morocco, Algeria and Saudi Arabia (Al-Yahya’ei et al. 2011; Bouamri et al. 2014; Khirani et al. 2020; [SOURCE: Page_239]

Harkousse et al. 2021a). Arbuscular mycorrhizae assessment is mostly done under microscope by [SOURCE: Page_239]

histological observation of the fungal structures inside the roots (Brundrett et al. 1996), and this was [SOURCE: Page_239]

done on cultivated date palms (e.g. Bouamri et al. 2006; Zougari-elwedi et al. 2016; Dalal and Solanki [SOURCE: Page_239]

2021). These studies along with others on date palm seedlings (Ramoliya and Pandey 2003; Abohatem [SOURCE: Page_239]

et al. 2011; Arab et al. 2016), unveils high mycorrhizal rates and infection capacities for date palms in [SOURCE: Page_239]

both controlled and field conditions. This potential of mycorrhization, however, has not been assessed [SOURCE: Page_239]

in date palms growing out of farms, in natural conditions. [SOURCE: Page_239]

157 [SOURCE: Page_239]

Mycorrhization does not solely depend on the host plant, as environmental factors also strongly [SOURCE: Page_240]

influence the survivability and the mycorrhizal potential of AMF. For instance, non-mycorrhizal plants [SOURCE: Page_240]

are more diverse and common in arid lands (Brundrett and Tedersoo 2018; Soudzilovskaia et al. 2020), [SOURCE: Page_240]

while desert plants show low levels of arbuscular mycorrhizae colonization in roots (Treseder and Cross [SOURCE: Page_240]

2006). Arid soils present numerous adverse conditions for AMF, including drought, salinity, high pH [SOURCE: Page_240]

values, sand abrasivity, heat and low plant cover (Apple 2010; Soudzilovskaia et al. 2017; Vasar et al. [SOURCE: Page_240]

2021; Adenan et al. 2021). Arbuscular mycorrhizae is mostly known for its role on phosphorus [SOURCE: Page_240]

nutrition, thus, high concentrations of P in soils often reduce significantly the mycorrhizal potential [SOURCE: Page_240]

(George et al. 1995; Ma et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_240]

In this study, we question the arbuscular mycorrhizal status of the date palm and the factors that may [SOURCE: Page_240]

influence this mycorrhization at the scale of the hyperarid, hyperalkaline region of AlUla, Northwest [SOURCE: Page_240]

Saudi Arabia. We observed and measured the arbuscular mycorrhizae in roots of more than 100 date [SOURCE: Page_240]

palms collected in six different farm types and four soil water systems. We also analyzed the soil [SOURCE: Page_240]

geochemical properties to understand how they may affect the mycorrhizal potential of this crop. [SOURCE: Page_240]

Companion plants (collected close to date palms) in AlUla and date palms from another region, [SOURCE: Page_240]

Khaybar, located 170 km Southeast from AlUla, were collected to characterize the specificity of the [SOURCE: Page_240]

mycorrhization of date palm in AlUla. [SOURCE: Page_240]

2 Material and method [SOURCE: Page_240]

2.1 Area of study [SOURCE: Page_240]

The study region was located in the hyperarid region of AlUla, Northwest Saudi Arabia. This region is [SOURCE: Page_240]

characterized by a hyperarid climate with temperatures exceeding 45°C in summer and uncommon [SOURCE: Page_240]

rainfall, generally one per year with 30-170 mm/year (Toumi et al. 2015). The region accounts for more [SOURCE: Page_240]

than 60,000 people, most of whom live in the city of AlUla. The rest of the population is mainly [SOURCE: Page_240]

distributed across farms outside the city, and in Bedouin nomadic habitat. East of AlUla is the Sharaan [SOURCE: Page_240]

158 [SOURCE: Page_240]

Nature Reserve, which accesses are now restricted. In this region, the date palm is the most important [SOURCE: Page_241]

crop and can be cultivated and found in a variety of conditions. A previous prospect by Gros- [SOURCE: Page_241]

Balthazard, Battesti et al. (2023) categorized the types of farms in which the date palms are cultivated [SOURCE: Page_241]

in AlUla. These categories were: i) Old farms: historical palm groves mainly located inside the city with [SOURCE: Page_241]

mostly layered polyculture ii) Abandoned: Among the Old farms, they form the fractions of these farms [SOURCE: Page_241]

that are no longer cultivated, irrigated and harvested. iii) Bedouin palm groves: cultivated and [SOURCE: Page_241]

harvested by Bedouin, pastoral nomad social group, located outside of the sedentary areas with [SOURCE: Page_241]

variable labor investment levels from solely harvesting to small gardens. iv) Modern 20th century farms: [SOURCE: Page_241]

farms developed outside the historical area of the city with exportation objectives, characterized by [SOURCE: Page_241]

monoculture of mostly one single elite variety, even-aged trees planted in line. v) New 21st century [SOURCE: Page_241]

farms: developed recently outside of the city, with diversification of cultural practices, date palm [SOURCE: Page_241]

cultivars, crops (e.g. Citrus, Moringa) and sometimes irrigation practices and systems. vi) Natural date [SOURCE: Page_241]

palms: these date palms grow in uncultivated areas and are probably not harvested and do not receive [SOURCE: Page_241]

any kind of man investment nor irrigation, the so-called feral date palms. [SOURCE: Page_241]

Date palms also grow in different soil water systems in irrigated and non-irrigated conditions. The main [SOURCE: Page_241]

irrigation method is the flooding which consists on bringing large amounts of water to the base of the [SOURCE: Page_241]

date palm. Some rare farms experiment the surface drip irrigation which consists on bringing water in [SOURCE: Page_241]

longer period through pierced pipes. The other categories are the abandoned farms, which were [SOURCE: Page_241]

previously irrigated by the flooding method but have not been irrigated for several years; and the [SOURCE: Page_241]

natural date palms that only receive water from the scarce rains and the underground stocks. [SOURCE: Page_241]

Additionally, samplings were done in the nearby region of Khaybar located 170 km Southeast of AlUla, [SOURCE: Page_241]

for comparison with the date palms in AlUla. [SOURCE: Page_241]

159 [SOURCE: Page_241]

2.2 Sampling [SOURCE: Page_242]

In total, 105 date palms were sampled in a wide variety of locations, farm types, according to previous [SOURCE: Page_242]

prospection by Gros-Balthazard, Battesti et al. (2023), and irrigation conditions in AlUla, plus twelve [SOURCE: Page_242]

samples in Khaybar. On each collected date palm, the irrigation system was noted. Three to five roots [SOURCE: Page_242]

were collected at 40 cm deep. Roots were also collected at different sampling depths by digging pits [SOURCE: Page_242]

on eight date palms collected in AlUla (two in each soil water system) with three sampling depths (40, [SOURCE: Page_242]

80 and 140 cm), and three sampling areas at each depth, for a total of nine samples per date palm to [SOURCE: Page_242]

search for arbuscular mycorrhization at deeper depths. Additionally, the roots of 32 companion plants [SOURCE: Page_242]

(29 in AlUla and three in Khaybar) growing at the base of some date palms were collected for [SOURCE: Page_242]

mycorrhizal assessment. Companion plants were identified using reference work (Migahid 1996; [SOURCE: Page_242]

Chaudhary 1999; Collenette 1999). At each root sampling, soil was collected and sieved through a 2 [SOURCE: Page_242]

mm mesh for chemical analyses. [SOURCE: Page_242]

2.3 Soil chemical analyses [SOURCE: Page_242]

All soils were analyzed for pHH2O, pHKCl and acidification potentials and their atomic composition were [SOURCE: Page_242]

assessed with X-Ray Fluorescence. Soil pH were measured with a Knick 766 pH Meter (Knick [SOURCE: Page_242]

International, Berlin, Germany) in 1:5 (v:v) of water or KCl (1 mol.L -1) solution, after mixing and [SOURCE: Page_242]

decanting. The acidification potential is the difference between pH H2O and pHKCl. Atomic abundances [SOURCE: Page_242]

of elements from magnesium to uranium in soil were measured using the XRF S$_1$ Titan analyzer [SOURCE: Page_242]

(Bruker, Billerica, Massachusetts, USA). Each soil was placed and stamped in three plastic capsules [SOURCE: Page_242]

topped with prolene film and each capsule was measured three times by XRF analyzer. The nine [SOURCE: Page_242]

measures for each element were averaged. Major elements that varied across samples were selected [SOURCE: Page_242]

and considered as variables: magnesium (Mg), aluminum (Al), silicon (Si), phosphorus (P), sulphur (S), [SOURCE: Page_242]

calcium (Ca), manganese (Mn). For the analyses, the XRF values were centered log ratio transformed [SOURCE: Page_242]

due to the compositional nature of the data. [SOURCE: Page_242]

160 [SOURCE: Page_242]

2.4 Mycorrhizal assessment by histology [SOURCE: Page_243]

For each date palm and each date palm companion plants, 3 to 5 roots were used to search for [SOURCE: Page_243]

arbuscular mycorrhizal structures. Roots were rinsed with water then cleared with a 10% KOH solution [SOURCE: Page_243]

at 90°C for ~1h (time depending on the diameter and tannins load of the root). Roots were rinsed then [SOURCE: Page_243]

let in acidic water (~pH 3) for 2 h, then, they were stained using Trypan blue staining in Amman [SOURCE: Page_243]

lactophenol solution at 96°C for 10 min according to Phillips and Hayman (1970). Roots were cut into [SOURCE: Page_243]

5-20 fragments of 1 cm depending on available material and observed under microscope. Mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_243]

status of the individual (single plant) was confirmed if characteristic structures of AMF mycorrhizae [SOURCE: Page_243]

(arbuscules, vesicles or AMF spores) were observed. When the individual was colonized with AMF [SOURCE: Page_243]

𝑁𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟 𝑜𝑓 𝑐𝑜𝑙𝑜𝑛𝑖𝑧𝑒𝑑 𝑓𝑟𝑎𝑔𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑠 structures, the frequency was calculated as           𝑇𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟 𝑜𝑓 𝑓𝑟𝑎𝑔𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑠 ∗ 100. Intensity of [SOURCE: Page_243]

mycorrhization was visually assessed in percentage using the intensity scale of Trouvelot et al. (1986). [SOURCE: Page_243]

2.5 Statistical analyses [SOURCE: Page_243]

All data analyses and figures were made in R (version 4.3.0, R Core Team, 2023). To visualize the [SOURCE: Page_243]

possible role of geochemical parameters in the presence of mycorrhization in date palm roots, all [SOURCE: Page_243]

geochemical parameters (pH and XRF data) were fitted in a principal component analysis (PCA). A [SOURCE: Page_243]

generalized linear model (GLM) was used to determine which parameters were significantly (p < 0.05) [SOURCE: Page_243]

correlated with mycorrhization of date palms. The efficiency of the model to predict mycorrhization of [SOURCE: Page_243]

date palm based on soil conditions was then tested on the same database used to create it. The [SOURCE: Page_243]

differences between the mycorrhizal and non-mycorrhizal date palms in the parameters statistically [SOURCE: Page_243]

relevant in the GLM were analyzed with an analysis of variance (ANOVA) followed by a Tukey test. All [SOURCE: Page_243]

of the above analyses were done without the root samples from Khaybar as those were used for [SOURCE: Page_243]

comparison with the samples of AlUla. For the comparison, a PCA was made and the same soil [SOURCE: Page_243]

parameters were compared with ANOVAs. [SOURCE: Page_243]

161 [SOURCE: Page_243]

3 Results [SOURCE: Page_244]

3.1 Overall mycorrhization of date palms in AlUla [SOURCE: Page_244]

The observation of the date palm roots revealed that only 20% (21/105) of the date palms were found [SOURCE: Page_244]

colonized by arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi. The mean frequency among colonized date palms was 56% [SOURCE: Page_244]

(min = 6%, max = 100%, sd = 32.5%) and the mean intensity 4% (min = 0.5%, max = 35%, sd = 11.4%). [SOURCE: Page_244]

Among the 21 date palms with arbuscular mycorrhizal structures, none were found with arbuscules [SOURCE: Page_244]

(Fig. 1 a-d). [SOURCE: Page_244]

Fig.1 Examples of mycorrhizal structures in date palm roots (a-d) and in adventitious plant roots (e-h). [SOURCE: Page_244]

e: Picris cyanocarpa (Asteraceae); f: Sonchus oleraceus (Asteraceae); g: Unidentified Poaceae; h: [SOURCE: Page_244]

Unidentified Solanaceae. The yellow bar corresponds to 50 µm. Arbuscular mycorrhizal structures are [SOURCE: Page_244]

pointed with red arrows, V = Vesicles, H = Hyphae, A = Arbuscules, S = Spores. [SOURCE: Page_244]

162 [SOURCE: Page_244]

Regarding the pits (the samples made at three different depths), no mycorrhization traces were found [SOURCE: Page_245]

in the roots harvested at any of the depths in any of the pits. It seems that the date palms with [SOURCE: Page_245]

mycorrhization are dispatched on the whole map (except in the Sharaan Nature reserve) and shows [SOURCE: Page_245]

no clear geographical pattern (Fig.2) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Fig.2 Map of the date palm samples with presence or absence of mycorrhizae. The top map shows [SOURCE: Page_245]

the region of AlUla while the bottom map shows the samples from Khaybar. Each blue or green dot [SOURCE: Page_245]

correspond to one sampled date palm. Red points represent the center of the samples when the [SOURCE: Page_245]

points overlap due to geographical proximity. [SOURCE: Page_245]

163 [SOURCE: Page_245]

3.2 Mycorrhization of companion plants [SOURCE: Page_246]

In AlUla, 83% (24/29) of the companion plants collected close to date palms were found with [SOURCE: Page_246]

arbuscular mycorrhizal structures (Table 1). The mean frequency was 89% (min = 33%, max = 100%, sd [SOURCE: Page_246]

= 22.0%) and the mean intensity 14% (min = 1%, max = 93%, sd = 20.4%). The roots of the companion [SOURCE: Page_246]

plants often displayed arbuscules (Fig. 1 e-h). Companion plants were collected close to some of the [SOURCE: Page_246]

date palms, among those, mycorrhizal companion plants were found next to 14 non-mycorrhizal date [SOURCE: Page_246]

palms and five mycorrhizal date palms. [SOURCE: Page_246]

Table. 1 Mycorrhizal status, frequency and intensity of mycorrhization in roots of companion plants [SOURCE: Page_246]

species in the regions of AlUla and Khaybar. The number of individuals for a species is indicated (n). [SOURCE: Page_246]

Frequency and intensity are calculated only for mycorrhizal individuals. [SOURCE: Page_246]

3.3 Influence of farm types and soil water systems on date palm [SOURCE: Page_247]

arbuscular mycorrhizae [SOURCE: Page_247]

The mycorrhizal date palms were unevenly represented among types of farms in AlUla. The highest [SOURCE: Page_247]

percentage of mycorrhizal date palms were in “new 21st century farms” (35% of the 43 date palms) [SOURCE: Page_247]

and in “Bedouin farms” (33% of the 12 date palms) (Fig. 3 a). Of the six “new 21 st century farm” sites [SOURCE: Page_247]

sampled, four had mycorrhizal date palms. Among the “Bedouin farm” sites, three out of four featured [SOURCE: Page_247]

mycorrhizal date palms, while they represented two out of three “modern 20 st century farm” sites. [SOURCE: Page_247]

For the different soil water systems, the highest percentage of mycorrhizal date palms (38%) was found [SOURCE: Page_247]

in drip irrigated farms, although only two of these farms were collected and the mycorrhizal date palms [SOURCE: Page_247]

were found in only one of them. 25% of the date palm individuals found in flooding irrigated farms [SOURCE: Page_247]

showed mycorrhizal structures, this represented six out of twelve farm sites. Only one (of a total of 10 [SOURCE: Page_247]

date palms) date palm tree was found mycorrhizal in natural conditions. [SOURCE: Page_247]

Fig. 3 Repartition of mycorrhizal and non-mycorrhizal date palms among the different farm types (a) [SOURCE: Page_248]

and the different soil water systems (b). The percentage of date palms with mycorrhization traces (yes) [SOURCE: Page_248]

in each condition is indicated above the bars. [SOURCE: Page_248]

3.4 Effect of geochemical parameters on date palm mycorrhization [SOURCE: Page_248]

Soils collected around date palm roots in AlUla were sandy, dominated by silicon (> 82%) and [SOURCE: Page_248]

hyperalkaline with a mean pHH2O of 8.24 ± 0.45. The PCA did not show major differences between the [SOURCE: Page_248]

geochemical parameters of soils of mycorrhizal date palms and non-mycorrhizal date palms as the [SOURCE: Page_248]

ellipses overlapped extensively (Fig. 4). [SOURCE: Page_248]

166 [SOURCE: Page_248]

Fig. 4 Principal Component Analysis (PCA) representing the contribution of soil geochemical [SOURCE: Page_249]

parameters (pH and XRF, clr transformed, measures). Samples are colored according to land uses. [SOURCE: Page_249]

Vectors are colored according to their level of contributions to each dimension. [SOURCE: Page_249]

The parameters were fitted into a generalized linear model (GLM) and retrieved that only the pH [SOURCE: Page_249]

explained significantly (p = 0.0084) the differences in soil parameters between mycorrhizal and non- [SOURCE: Page_249]

mycorrhizal date palms (Table S$_1$). However, two other parameters had p values < 0,1, Aluminum (p = [SOURCE: Page_249]

0.091) and Phosphorus (p= 0.069). These three parameters were compared between the mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_249]

and non-mycorrhizal date palms (Fig. 5). ANOVA followed by Tukey tests showed significant differences [SOURCE: Page_249]

in pHH2O (p = 0.00043) and phosphorus (p = 0.043) between the two groups, while aluminum did not [SOURCE: Page_249]

show significant differences (p = 0.33). The GLM was tested on the same data used to create it (date [SOURCE: Page_249]

palms of AlUla) and was efficient at 83% to predict the mycorrhization of date palms. [SOURCE: Page_249]

167 [SOURCE: Page_249]

Fig. 5 Geochemical differences (pHH2O, Aluminum and Phosphorus) in soils of mycorrhizal (Yes) and non- [SOURCE: Page_250]

mycorrhizal (No) date palms. The data for Al and P were clr transformed. Different letters indicate [SOURCE: Page_250]

significantly different values. [SOURCE: Page_250]

3.5 Comparison between date palms of AlUla and Khaybar [SOURCE: Page_250]

Date palms in Khaybar were collected in two “abandoned farms” and one “new (21 st century) farm” to [SOURCE: Page_250]

compare their mycorrhization with those of AlUla. 66% (8/12) of the date palms collected in Khaybar [SOURCE: Page_250]

had traces of arbuscular mycorrhization in their roots with a mean frequency of 75% and a mean [SOURCE: Page_250]

intensity of 14%. All of the individuals (5) collected in abandoned farms, were mycorrhizal while 43% [SOURCE: Page_250]

(3/7) of the date palms collected in the new farm in Khaybar were mycorrhizal (Fig. 3 a). [SOURCE: Page_250]

In terms of soils parameters, the soils of Khaybar did not show significant differences compared to soils [SOURCE: Page_250]

of AlUla in pHH2O (p = 0.67) but showed significant lower aluminum (p = 1*10-7) and phosphorus (p = [SOURCE: Page_250]

0.021) values (Fig. 6). The PCA shows that date palms of Khaybar forms a group inside the AlUla ellipse [SOURCE: Page_250]

indicating no major difference with the soils of AlUla (Fig. S$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_250]

168 [SOURCE: Page_250]

Fig. 6 Geochemical differences (pHH2O, Aluminum and Phosphorus) in soils of date palms collected in [SOURCE: Page_251]

AlUla and Khaybar. The data for Al and P were clr transformed. Different letters indicate significantly [SOURCE: Page_251]

different values. [SOURCE: Page_251]

4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_251]

4.1 The unusually low mycorrhizal presence in date palm of AlUla [SOURCE: Page_251]

Our study shows that only 20% of the analyzed date palms in AlUla were found to be mycorrhizal, [SOURCE: Page_251]

compared to 66% in the Khaybar region. The date palm was commonly described in other studies with [SOURCE: Page_251]

high mycorrhizal rates. For instance, 100% of the date palms collected in farms in Morocco by (Bouamri [SOURCE: Page_251]

et al. 2006, 2014) and in India by (Dalal and Solanki 2021) were found mycorrhizal. Date palms of the [SOURCE: Page_251]

AlUla region, thus, show unusually low mycorrhizal rates. [SOURCE: Page_251]

The pits were all done on date palms that happened to be non-mycorrhizal. Among the 9 samples done [SOURCE: Page_251]

on each of these date palms, none were mycorrhizal implying that root mycorrhizationt was not [SOURCE: Page_251]

affected by the depth. The shallower samples, however, were collected approximately at 40 cm deep, [SOURCE: Page_251]

to avoid the topsoil fraction, a usually changing and biologically active fraction of the soil (Darmody et [SOURCE: Page_251]

169 [SOURCE: Page_251]

hypothesize that roots nearer the surface might show higher mycorrhizal rates, however [SOURCE: Page_252]

physicochemical properties of the soils were observed to be rather homogenic along the depth [SOURCE: Page_252]

gradient. When mycorrhized, the frequency of date palm mycorrhization (mean = 56% range = 6 – [SOURCE: Page_252]

100%) is in line with the results of Bouamri et al. (2014) (7 - 60% of root length colonization) but lower [SOURCE: Page_252]

than in Bouamri et al. (2006) (72.5 – 100% of frequency). As for the Intensity of colonization, we [SOURCE: Page_252]

measured a very low intensity mean (mean = 4%, corresponding to 1 – 2 on the scale of Trouvelot et [SOURCE: Page_252]

al. (1986), range = 0.5 – 35%) which is lower than what was measured by Bouamri et al. (2006) (5.26 – [SOURCE: Page_252]

43 %) in farms in Morocco. In a field study, Lucic-Mercy et al. (2017) noted a very low arbuscular [SOURCE: Page_252]

mycorrhization susceptibility of the species Phoenix reclinata. [SOURCE: Page_252]

Additionally, the companion plants collected close to the date palms showed high mycorrhizal rates, [SOURCE: Page_252]

even at the base of non-mycorrhizal date palms indicating that the soils have the potential to [SOURCE: Page_252]

selectively form arbuscular mycorrhizae with other plants. [SOURCE: Page_252]

4.2 Arbuscular mycorrhization is variable among the farming practices [SOURCE: Page_252]

Among the factors that can influence arbuscular mycorrhizae, cultivation conditions play a significant [SOURCE: Page_252]

role. This term involves a mix of practices (e.g. weeding strategies, irrigation strategies, use of [SOURCE: Page_252]

fertilizers, pesticides, monoculture) that are each known to individually affect mycorrhization [SOURCE: Page_252]

(Schreiner et al. 2007; Trinchera and Warren Raffa 2023). In our conditions, the farm type with the [SOURCE: Page_252]

highest rate of mycorrhizal date palms (35%) were new 21st century farms. These exploitations utilize [SOURCE: Page_252]

farming approaches and strategies that may imply experimenting with new irrigation system, less use [SOURCE: Page_252]

of weeding, application of biofertilizers, polyculture and diversification of cultivars. The six farms [SOURCE: Page_252]

collected on this farm type showcased different practices each time. They all differed however from [SOURCE: Page_252]

the practices characteristic of the modern 20th century farms, i.e monoculture, abundant watering [SOURCE: Page_252]

and fertilization, intense weeding. Land use intensity was found to reduce the mycorrhizal potential of [SOURCE: Page_252]

170 [SOURCE: Page_252]

soils in Europe (Oehl et al. 2003), while AMF colonization was lower in oil palm roots growing in [SOURCE: Page_253]

monoculture than in agroforestry conditions (da Silva Maia et al. 2021). [SOURCE: Page_253]

New 21st century farm types may be seen as an attempt to return to old farming ways, like the ones [SOURCE: Page_253]

that were done in the old farms (crop and cultivar diversity), taking distances from practices of modern [SOURCE: Page_253]

20th century exploitations but with the aim of keeping the high yields obtained with the latter. Old [SOURCE: Page_253]

farms, however, just like abandoned farms and natural ecosystems, showed no traces of AMF [SOURCE: Page_253]

colonization in any of the collected date palm roots. In contrast, in Khaybar, all the date palms collected [SOURCE: Page_253]

in abandoned farms were mycorrhizal. To the best of our knowledge, this study is the first one [SOURCE: Page_253]

searching for mycorrhizal presence in roots of date palms in natural ecosystems. [SOURCE: Page_253]

The Bedouin farming practices are rarely documented (Nabhan 2007; Gros-Balthazard et al, Battesti. [SOURCE: Page_253]

2023), but they involve a variety of practices from gardening to harvesting. Among the Bedouin sites [SOURCE: Page_253]

we collected, one was composed of two date palms growing at the top of a rocky hill. This site had [SOURCE: Page_253]

every characteristic of a natural ecosystem, but was considered harvested by Bedouin populations by [SOURCE: Page_253]

(Gros-Balthazard, Battesti et al. 2023) and thus was noted as a Bedouin farm. Among these two date [SOURCE: Page_253]

palms one was found with mycorrhizal traces. [SOURCE: Page_253]

Regarding the irrigation practices, drip irrigated date palms had slightly higher mycorrhizal rates than [SOURCE: Page_253]

the other systems. The prevalence of arbuscular mycorrhizae in drip irrigated date palms is in [SOURCE: Page_253]

opposition with results of Deng et al. (2022) who measured a decrease in the abundance of AMF taxa [SOURCE: Page_253]

in the rhizosphere of alfalfa grown with drip irrigation compared to flooding irrigation. However, the [SOURCE: Page_253]

presence of AMF taxa in the rhizosphere is not a proxy of arbuscular mycorrhizae. All the drip irrigated [SOURCE: Page_253]

farms were in new 21st century farms which could be an explanation of this increased rate. [SOURCE: Page_253]

171 [SOURCE: Page_253]

4.3 pH, aluminum and phosphorus are major drivers of date palm [SOURCE: Page_254]

mycorrhization in this hyperalkaline region. [SOURCE: Page_254]

In a larger study involving more soil samples representatives of the diversity of environment in the [SOURCE: Page_254]

region, a mean pH of 8.86 was measured (Robin-Soriano et al. 2024a). The soils collected close to date [SOURCE: Page_254]

palm roots were thus more acidic with a pH of 8.24. Plants are known to be able to influence their [SOURCE: Page_254]

direct environment through the exudation of enzymes, primary and secondary metabolites and ions [SOURCE: Page_254]

to impact on the microbial communities or to modify the pH of their rhizosphere (Hinsinger et al. 2003; [SOURCE: Page_254]

Rolfe et al. 2019). This modification in pH, helps the plant to compensate the ionic unbalance at the [SOURCE: Page_254]

soil root interface, participating on increasing the nutrient uptake. In alkaline soils such as those of [SOURCE: Page_254]

AlUla, plants can secrete carbonic acids to reduce soil pH (Hinsinger et al. 2003). Soil pH can also be [SOURCE: Page_254]

affected by farming practices like the use of some fertilizers (e.g. phosphoric acid) although it was [SOURCE: Page_254]

difficult to gather fertilization information from farmers in AlUla. We analyzed that the pH was the [SOURCE: Page_254]

main explainer of differences between mycorrhizal and non-mycorrhizal date palms and that the soils [SOURCE: Page_254]

of roots of mycorrhizal date palms were significantly more acidic than those of non-mycorrhizal date [SOURCE: Page_254]

palms. The pH is known to influence the microbial communities including AMF communities with a [SOURCE: Page_254]

negative effect of soil pH on AMF species richness (Adenan et al. 2021). Studies are lacking, however, [SOURCE: Page_254]

on the direct capacity of mycorrhization of plants in different controlled soil pH conditions. [SOURCE: Page_254]

Nonetheless, mycorrhization can occur even in soils with very high pH (> 10) (Janardhanan et al. 1994), [SOURCE: Page_254]

and mycorrhization of date palm has been observed in soil pH of 8.70 (Bouamri et al. 2006). [SOURCE: Page_254]

The other parameters that were shown to have a probable impact on the mycorrhization are [SOURCE: Page_254]

phosphorus and aluminum content in the soils. Aluminum in these soils has been shown to be [SOURCE: Page_254]

representative of the percentage of clay. Clay soils are more compact and have better water and [SOURCE: Page_254]

nutrient retention capacities (Kome et al. 2019) thus, are theoretically more suitable for mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_254]

development. In our analyses aluminum was negatively linked to mycorrhization inducing that [SOURCE: Page_254]

mycorrhizal date palms were more present in soils with less clay, thus, more sandy soils. This is in [SOURCE: Page_254]

172 [SOURCE: Page_254]

opposition with the results of (Bitterlich et al. 2024) on maize where highest root colonization was [SOURCE: Page_255]

found with high clay contents. On the other hand, we did not find any significant differences in [SOURCE: Page_255]

aluminum content between soils where mycorrhizal and non-mycorrhizal date palm roots were [SOURCE: Page_255]

harvested. [SOURCE: Page_255]

There was also a significant difference in soil phosphorus content between mycorrhizal and non- [SOURCE: Page_255]

mycorrhizal date palms. Phosphorus content in soil was found to negatively influence mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_255]

occurrence. This result is in line with main literature as phosphorus is a very important predictor known [SOURCE: Page_255]

to reduce AMF diversity and arbuscular mycorrhization (Plenchette and Morel 1996; Ma et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_255]

Indeed, arbuscular mycorrhizae is well-known for its positive effect on phosphorus nutrition of plants, [SOURCE: Page_255]

thus for the abundance of mycorrhizal plants in phosphorus depleted soils. Soils of AlUla are desertic [SOURCE: Page_255]

and hold low concentration of nutrients, including phosphorus. This is also true for cultivated soils of [SOURCE: Page_255]

farms. It is probable that some farms uses fertilizers and/or biofertilizers, however the presence of [SOURCE: Page_255]

phosphorus in the soils is always very scarce. [SOURCE: Page_255]

Soils of Khaybar differed from those of AlUla by displaying lower concentration of aluminum and [SOURCE: Page_255]

phosphorus content but not pH, while mycorrhization of date palms was higher in Khaybar than in [SOURCE: Page_255]

AlUla. This could be an indication that micro differences in phosphorus content in soils, even in [SOURCE: Page_255]

phosphorus scarce soils can influence the mycorrhization of date palm. [SOURCE: Page_255]

4.4 Does arbuscular mycorrhizae holds interests for date palm [SOURCE: Page_255]

cultivation in AlUla? [SOURCE: Page_255]

In this study we analyzed that mycorrhization was scarce in roots of date palms of AlUla and almost [SOURCE: Page_255]

absent in date palms sampled in natural conditions. Arbuscular mycorrhization holds numerous [SOURCE: Page_255]

advantages for plants in arid soils, i.e increased tolerance to salinity, drought, improved water and [SOURCE: Page_255]

nutrient absorption and increased yields (Apple 2010; Tyagi et al. 2017; Ou-Zine et al. 2023b). In [SOURCE: Page_255]

another hand, plants in arid soils have lower number of fine roots (Treseder and Cross 2006), [SOURCE: Page_255]

173 [SOURCE: Page_255]

mycorrhization is reduced in dry soils (Baslam et al. 2014) and sandy soils are abrasive and can be [SOURCE: Page_256]

deleterious to hyphae. The relation between the AMF and the host plant involves nutrients exchanges, [SOURCE: Page_256]

notably, the plant gives a portion of its photosynthates to the fungi. Photosynthesis process involves [SOURCE: Page_256]

the use of water, a scarce resource in these environments, implying that if the fungi does not provide [SOURCE: Page_256]

enough water to the plant, the trade-off could be deleterious to the plant which could lead to an [SOURCE: Page_256]

interruption of the symbiosis (Chaves et al. 2002). [SOURCE: Page_256]

We also reveal that no arbuscules were found in the examined roots. This finding is in line with the [SOURCE: Page_256]

results of Dreyer et al. (2010) unveiling constant presence of arbuscular mycorrhization but absence [SOURCE: Page_256]

of arbuscules in P. dactylifera and P. canariensis. The definition of “what should be considered [SOURCE: Page_256]

arbuscular mycorrhizae?” is still under debate (Bueno et al. 2021; Soudzilovskaia et al. 2022). One [SOURCE: Page_256]

aspect of the debate lies in the recognition as an arbuscular mycorrhizae of root colonization without [SOURCE: Page_256]

arbuscules. It is recognized that AMF can colonize and form structures in non-mycorrhizal plants and [SOURCE: Page_256]

mutants, but it is also widely considered that presence of vesicles in the roots might be a proof of the [SOURCE: Page_256]

symbiosis. In our study we considered that presence of at least vesicles or AMF spores (most of the [SOURCE: Page_256]

time with hyphae) in the roots were proof of arbuscular mycorrhizae. In the case where we consider [SOURCE: Page_256]

that the absence of arbuscules is an indicator of an absence of arbuscular mycorrhizae, then we can [SOURCE: Page_256]

affirm that none of the date palm roots that we collected and analyzed were mycorrhizal. In other [SOURCE: Page_256]

studies, however, arbuscules were frequently observed in date palms in both controlled and farm [SOURCE: Page_256]

conditions (Bouamri et al. 2014; El Kinany et al. 2019). [SOURCE: Page_256]

Mere presence of mycorrhizal structures in the roots (and even more in absence of arbuscules), [SOURCE: Page_256]

however, cannot be an affirmation of the effectivity of the symbiosis (Kuyper and Jansa 2023). We can [SOURCE: Page_256]

confirm that most of the roots we collected did not show mycorrhizal traces at the time of collection [SOURCE: Page_256]

and under the given conditions. The question of the generalization of these few observations of roots [SOURCE: Page_256]

(even if the pits had more sampled roots at different depths) to the whole date palm tree along the [SOURCE: Page_256]

year or across years, is valid. It is hard to conclude on the mycorrhizal status of a date palm, but we [SOURCE: Page_256]

174 [SOURCE: Page_256]

can, however, assess that mycorrhization was very low for date palms in the AlUla region compared to [SOURCE: Page_257]

bibliography and that we did not observe arbuscules in the examined roots. [SOURCE: Page_257]

Mycorrhization of date palm in AlUla can also be due to an absence of favorable AMF taxa in the soils [SOURCE: Page_257]

that enables an efficient symbiosis. Indeed, the arbuscular mycorrhizae can be very dependent on the [SOURCE: Page_257]

genotypes of both the plant and the fungi even though AMF are considered to be non or low-selective [SOURCE: Page_257]

(van der Heijden et al. 2015). Both the plants and the fungi have the ability to select and reward the [SOURCE: Page_257]

beneficial symbionts, and the efficiency of the symbiosis (as benefits for the plant) is very dependent [SOURCE: Page_257]

on the fungal partner’s genotype (van der Heijden et al. 2015). Roots of a part of the date palms [SOURCE: Page_257]

examined in the present study were analyzed by metabarcoding in (Robin-Soriano et al. 2024b). This [SOURCE: Page_257]

study unveiled that most AMF taxa identified in the date palm roots were assigned to genus of the [SOURCE: Page_257]

Glomeraceae family, i.e. Rhizophagus, Glomus, Sclerocystis, Septoglomus and Funneliformis. Glomus is [SOURCE: Page_257]

a commonly described by spore identification in mycorrhizal date palms (Bouamri et al. 2006, 2014) [SOURCE: Page_257]

but they also identified Scutellospora and Acaulospora which were not detected in our previous study. [SOURCE: Page_257]

Glomus was commonly used as an inoculant and succeeded in forming arbuscular mycorrhizae with [SOURCE: Page_257]

date palm (Diatta et al. 2014; Meddich et al. 2015). However, metabarcoding of one barcode is rather [SOURCE: Page_257]

inefficient for assigning at the species level for fungi and even more for AMF (Tedersoo et al. 2022), [SOURCE: Page_257]

thus we lack information on the species associated with our date palm roots to see if these species [SOURCE: Page_257]

were previously found able to form arbuscular mycorrhizae with the date palm. [SOURCE: Page_257]

Finally, questions remain on the potential effectiveness of the arbuscular mycorrhizae (when [SOURCE: Page_257]

observed) on stress tolerance, nutrition and yield (Kuyper and Jansa 2023). AlUla, could thus be an [SOURCE: Page_257]

interesting laboratory to explore date palm arbuscular mycorrhizae performances as non-mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_257]

date palms seem to grow along with mycorrhizal companion plants and mycorrhizal date palms. It [SOURCE: Page_257]

could be possible to study further a small quantity of date palms in a farm to verify their mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_257]

status, maybe study the nutrient exchanges in the symbiosis, and assess if differences in mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_257]

status affect parameters such as yield. [SOURCE: Page_257]

175 [SOURCE: Page_257]

5 Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_258]

In this study we observed 105 roots of date palms in the region of AlUla and unveiled surprisingly low [SOURCE: Page_258]

mycorrhizal rates, even though numerous companion plants growing close to the date palms were [SOURCE: Page_258]

found with mycorrhizal structures in their roots. Mycorrhizal date palms were more abundant in farms [SOURCE: Page_258]

with more contemporary practices but were absent or almost absent of date palms in old farms, [SOURCE: Page_258]

abandoned farms and in date palms sampled in natural ecosystems. We revealed that the chances of [SOURCE: Page_258]

arbuscular mycorrhizae occur in roots of date palm were negatively affected by increasing pH, [SOURCE: Page_258]

phosphorus content and to a lesser extent aluminum content in the soils, which last element can be [SOURCE: Page_258]

linked to clay ratio in soils. The efficiency of the observed symbiosis is questioned as no arbuscules [SOURCE: Page_258]

were observed in any of the date palm roots and that mycorrhizal intensity was very low. Finally, we [SOURCE: Page_258]

analyze that AlUla could be an interesting region to explore the impact of controlled mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_258]

inoculation on the agronomic performances of date palms. [SOURCE: Page_258]
